### I / 一
===Original===

In the admirably appointed, white-enamelled, wickerworked, bemirrored lounge of the best hotel of that town Sylvia Tietjens sat in a wickerwork chair, not listening rather abstractedly to a staff-major who was lachrymosely and continuously begging her to leave her bedroom door unlocked that night. She said:
“I don’t know﻿ ﻿… Yes, perhaps﻿ ﻿… I don’t know﻿ ﻿…” And looked distantly into a bluish wall-mirror that, like all the rest, was framed with white-painted cork bark. She stiffened a little and said:
“There’s Christopher!”
The staff-major dropped his hat, his stick and his gloves. His black hair, which was without parting and heavy with some preparation of a glutinous kind, moved agitatedly on his scalp. He had been saying that Sylvia had ruined his life. Didn’t Sylvia know that she had ruined his life? But for her he might have married some pure young thing. Now he exclaimed:
“But what does he want?﻿ ﻿… Good God!﻿ ﻿… what does he want?”
“He wants,” Sylvia said, “to play the part of Jesus Christ.”
Major Perowne exclaimed:
“Jesus Christ!﻿ ﻿… But he’s the most foul-mouthed officer in the general’s command﻿ ﻿…”
“Well,” Sylvia said, “if you had married your pure young thing she’d have﻿ ﻿… What is it?﻿ ﻿… cuckolded you within nine months﻿ ﻿…”
Perowne shuddered a little at the word. He mumbled:
“I don’t see﻿ ﻿… It seems to be the other way﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh, no, it isn’t,” Sylvia said. “Think it over﻿ ﻿… Morally, *you’re* the husband﻿ ﻿… *Im*morally, I should say﻿ ﻿… Because he’s the man I want﻿ ﻿… He looks ill﻿ ﻿… Do hospital authorities always tell wives what is the matter with their husbands?”
From his angle in the chair from which he had half-emerged Sylvia seemed to him to be looking at a blank wall. “I don’t see him,” Perowne said.
“I can see him in the glass,” Sylvia said. “Look! From here you can see him.”
Perowne shuddered a little more.
“I don’t want to see him﻿ ﻿… I have to see him sometimes in the course of duty﻿ ﻿… I don’t like to﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia said:
“*You*,” in a tone of very deep contempt. “You only carry chocolate boxes to flappers﻿ ﻿… How can he come across you in the course of duty?﻿ ﻿… You’re not a *soldier*!”
Perowne said:
“But what are we going to do? What will *he* do?”
“I,” Sylvia answered, “shall tell the pageboy when he comes with his card to say that I’m engaged﻿ ﻿… I don’t know what *he’ll* do. Hit you, very likely﻿ ﻿… He’s looking at your back now﻿ ﻿…”
Perowne became rigid, sunk into his deep chair.
“But he *couldn’t*!” he exclaimed agitatedly. “You said that he was playing the part of Jesus Christ. Our Lord wouldn’t hit people in an hotel lounge﻿ ﻿…”
“Our Lord!” Sylvia said contemptuously. “What do you know about our Lord?﻿ ﻿… Our Lord was a gentleman﻿ ﻿… Christopher is playing at being our Lord calling on the woman taken in adultery﻿ ﻿… He’s giving me the social backing that his being my husband seems to him to call for.”
A one-armed, bearded maitre d’hôtel approached them through groups of armchairs arranged for tête-à-tête. He said:
“Pardon﻿ ﻿… I did not see madame at first﻿ ﻿…” And displayed a card on a salver. Without looking at it, Sylvia said:
“*Dites à ce monsieur*﻿ ﻿… that I am occupied.” The maitre d’hôtel moved austerely away.
“But he’ll smash me to pieces﻿ ﻿…” Perowne exclaimed. “What am I to do?﻿ ﻿… What the deuce am I to do?” There would have been no way of exit for him except across Tietjens’ face.
With her spine very rigid and the expression of a snake that fixes a bird, Sylvia gazed straight in front of her and said nothing until she exclaimed:
“For God’s sake leave off trembling﻿ ﻿… He would not do anything to a girl like you﻿ ﻿… He’s a man﻿ ﻿…” The wickerwork of Perowne’s chair had been crepitating as if it had been in a railway car. The sound ceased with a jerk﻿ ﻿… Suddenly she clenched both her hands and let out a hateful little breath of air between her teeth.
“By the immortal saints,” she exclaimed, “I swear I’ll make his wooden face wince yet.”
---
In the bluish looking-glass, a few minutes before, she had seen the agate-blue eyes of her husband, thirty feet away, over armchairs and between the fans of palms. He was standing, holding a riding-whip, looking rather clumsy in the uniform that did not suit him. Rather clumsy and worn out, but completely expressionless! He had looked straight into the reflection of her eyes and then looked away. He moved so that his profile was towards her, and continued gazing motionless at an elk’s head that decorated the space of wall above glazed doors giving into the interior of the hotel. The hotel servant approaching him, he had produced a card and had given it to the servant, uttering three words. She saw his lips move in the three words: Mrs. Christopher Tietjens. She said, beneath her breath:
“Damn his chivalry!﻿ ﻿… Oh, God damn his chivalry!” She knew what was going on in his mind. He had seen her, with Perowne, so he had neither come towards her nor directed the servant to where she sat. For fear of embarrassing her! He would leave it to her to come to him if she wished.
The servant, visible in the mirror, had come and gone deviously back, Tietjens still gazing at the elk’s head. He had taken the card and restored it to his pocketbook and then had spoken to the servant. The servant had shrugged his shoulders with the formal hospitality of his class and, with his shoulders still shrugged and his one hand pointing towards the inner door, had preceded Tietjens into the hotel. Not one line of Tietjens’ face had moved when he had received back his card. It had been then that Sylvia had sworn that she would yet make his wooden face wince﻿ ﻿…
His face was intolerable. Heavy; fixed. Not insolent, but simply gazing over the heads of all things and created beings, into a world too distant for them to enter﻿ ﻿… And yet it seemed to her, since he was so clumsy and worn out, almost not sporting to persecute him. It was like whipping a dying bulldog﻿ ﻿…
She sank back into her chair with a movement almost of discouragement. She said:
“He’s gone into the hotel﻿ ﻿…”
Perowne lurched agitatedly forward in his chair. He exclaimed that he was going. Then he sank discouragedly back again:
“No, I’m not,” he said, “I’m probably much safer here. I might run against him going out.”
“You’ve realized that my petticoats protect you,” Sylvia said contemptuously. “Of course, Christopher would never hit anyone in my presence.”
Major Perowne was interrupting her by asking:
“What’s he going to do? What’s he doing in the hotel?” Mrs. Tietjens said:
“Guess!” She added: “What would you do in similar circumstances?”
“Go and wreck your bedroom,” Perowne answered with promptitude. “It’s what I did when I found you had left Yssingueux.”
Sylvia said:
“Ah, that was what the place was called.”
Perowne groaned:
“You’re callous,” he said. “There’s no other word for it. Callous. That’s what you are.”
Sylvia asked absently why he called her callous at just that juncture. She was imagining Christopher stumping clumsily along the hotel corridor looking at bedrooms, and then giving the hotel servant a handsome tip to ensure that he should be put on the same floor as herself. She could almost hear his not disagreeable male voice that vibrated a little from the chest and made her vibrate.
Perowne was grumbling on. Sylvia was callous because she had forgotten the name of the Brittany hamlet in which they had spent three blissful weeks together, though she had left it so suddenly that all her outfit remained in the hotel.
“Well, it wasn’t any kind of a beanfeast for me.” Sylvia went on, when she again gave him her attention. “Good heavens!﻿ ﻿… Do you think it *would* be any kind of a beanfeast with you, *pour tout potage*? Why should I remember the name of the hateful place?”
Perowne said:
“Yssingueux-les-Pervenches, such a pretty name,” reproachfully.
“It’s no good,” Sylvia answered, “your trying to awaken sentimental memories in me. You will have to make me forget what you were like if you want to carry on with me﻿ ﻿… I’m stopping here and listening to your corncrake of a voice because I want to wait until Christopher goes out of the hotel﻿ ﻿… Then I am going to my room to tidy up for Lady Sachse’s party and you will sit here and wait for me.”
“I’m *not*,” Perowne said, “going to Lady Sachse’s. Why, *he* is going to be one of the principal witnesses to sign the marriage contract. And Old Campion and all the rest of the staff are going to be there﻿ ﻿… You don’t catch *me*﻿ ﻿… An unexpected prior engagement is my line. No fear.”
“You’ll come with me, my little man,” Sylvia said, “if you ever want to bask in my smile again﻿ ﻿… I’m not going to Lady Sachse’s alone, looking as if I couldn’t catch a man to escort me, under the eyes of half the French house of peers﻿ ﻿… If they’ve got a house of peers!﻿ ﻿… You don’t catch *me*﻿ ﻿… No fear!” she mimicked his creaky voice. “You can go away as soon as you’ve shown yourself as my escort﻿ ﻿…”
“But, good God!” Perowne cried out, “that’s just what I mustn’t do. Campion said that if he heard any more of my being seen about with you he would have me sent back to my beastly regiment. And my beastly regiment is in the trenches﻿ ﻿… You don’t see me in the trenches, do you?”
“I’d rather see you there than in my own room,” Sylvia said. “Any day!”
“Ah, there you are!” Perowne exclaimed with animation. “What guarantee have I that if I do what you want I shall bask in your smile as you call it? I’ve got myself into a most awful hole, bringing you here without papers. You never told me you hadn’t any papers. General O’Hara, the P.M., has raised a most awful strafe about it﻿ ﻿… And what have I got for it?﻿ ﻿… Not the ghost of a smile﻿ ﻿… And you should see old O’Hara’s purple face!﻿ ﻿… Someone woke him from his afternoon nap to report to him about your heinous case and he hasn’t recovered from the indigestion yet﻿ ﻿… Besides, he hates Tietjens﻿ ﻿… Tietjens is always chipping away at his military police﻿ ﻿… O’Hara’s lambs﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia was not listening, but she was smiling a slow smile at an inward thought. It maddened him.
“What’s your game?” he exclaimed. “Hell and hounds, what’s your game?﻿ ﻿… You can’t have come here to see﻿ ﻿… *him*. You don’t come here to see me, as far as I can see. Well then﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia looked round at him with all her eyes, wide open as if she had just awakened from a deep sleep.
“I didn’t know I was coming,” she said. “It came into my head to come suddenly. Ten minutes before I started. And I came. I didn’t know papers were wanted. I suppose I could have got them if I had wanted them﻿ ﻿… You never asked me if I had any papers. You just froze on to me and had me into your special carriage﻿ ﻿… I didn’t know you were coming.”
That seemed to Perowne the last insult. He exclaimed:
“Oh, damn it, Sylvia! you *must* have known﻿ ﻿… You were at the Quirks’ squash on Wednesday evening. And *they* knew. My best friends.”
“Since you ask for it,” she said, “I didn’t know﻿ ﻿… And I would not have come by that train if I had known you would be going by it. You force me to say rude things to you.” She added: “Why can’t you be more conciliatory?” to keep him quiet for a little. His jaw dropped down.
She was wondering where Christopher had got the money to pay for a bed at the hotel. Only a very short time before she had drawn all the balance of his banking account, except for a shilling. It was the middle of the month and he could not have drawn any more pay﻿ ﻿… That, of course, was a try on her part. He might be forced into remonstrating. In the same way she had tried on the accusation that he had carried off her sheets. It was sheer wilfulness, and when she looked again at his motionless features she knew that she had been rather stupid﻿ ﻿… But she was at the end of her tether: she had before now tried making accusations against her husband, but she had never tried inconveniencing him﻿ ﻿… Now she suddenly realized the full stupidity of which she had been guilty. He would know perfectly well that those petty frightfulnesses of hers were not in the least her note; so he would know, too, that each of them was just a try on. He would say: “She is trying to make me squeal. I’m damned if I will!”
She would have to adopt much more formidable methods. She said: “He shall﻿ ﻿… he shall﻿ ﻿… he *shall* come to heel.”
Major Perowne had now closed his jaw. He was reflecting. Once he mumbled: “More *conciliatory*! Holy smoke!”
She was feeling suddenly in spirits: it was the sight of Christopher had done it: the perfect assurance that they were going to live under the same roof again. She would have betted all she possessed and her immortal soul on the chance that he would not take up with the Wannop girl. And it would have been betting on a certainty!﻿ ﻿… But she had had no idea what their relations were to be, after the war. At first she had thought that they had parted for good when she had gone off from their flat at four o’clock in the morning. It had seemed logical. But, gradually, in retreat at Birkenhead, in the still, white, nun’s room, doubt had come upon her. It was one of the disadvantages of living as they did that they seldom spoke their thoughts. But that was also at times an advantage. She had certainly meant their parting to be for good. She had certainly raised her voice in giving the name of her station to the taxi-man with the pretty firm conviction that he would hear her; and she had been pretty well certain that he would take it as a sign that the breath had gone out of their union﻿ ﻿… Pretty certain. But not quite!﻿ ﻿…
She would have died rather than write to him; she would die, now, rather than give any inkling that she wanted them to live under the same roof again﻿ ﻿… She said to herself:
“Is he writing to that girl?” And then: “No!﻿ ﻿… I’m certain that he isn’t.”﻿ ﻿… She had had all his letters stopped at the flat, except for a few circulars that she let dribble through to him, so that he might imagine that all his correspondence was coming through. From the letters to him that she did read she was pretty sure that he had given no other address than the flat in Gray’s Inn﻿ ﻿… But there had been no letters from Valentine Wannop﻿ ﻿… Two from Mrs. Wannop, two from his brother Mark, one from Port Scatho, one or two from brother officers and some official chits﻿ ﻿… She said to herself that, if there *had* been any letters from that girl, she would have let all his letters go through, including the girl’s﻿ ﻿… Now she was not so certain that she would have.
In the glass she saw Christopher marching woodenly out of the hotel, along the path that led from door to door behind her﻿ ﻿… It came to her with extraordinary gladness﻿—the absolute conviction that he was not corresponding with Miss Wannop. The absolute conviction﻿ ﻿… If he had come alive enough to do that he would have looked different. She did not know how he would have looked. But different﻿ ﻿… Alive! Perhaps self-conscious: perhaps﻿ ﻿… satisfied﻿ ﻿…
For some time the major had been grumbling about his wrongs. He said that he followed her about all day, like a lapdog, and got nothing for it. Now she wanted him to be conciliatory. She said she wanted to have a man on show as escort. Well then, an escort got something﻿ ﻿… At just this moment he was beginning again with:
“Look here﻿ ﻿… will you let me come to your room tonight or will you not?”
She burst into high, loud laughter. He said:
“Damn it all, it isn’t any laughing matter!﻿ ﻿… Look here! You don’t know what I risk﻿ ﻿… There are A.P.M.s and P.M.s and deputy sub-acting A.P.M.s walking about the corridors of all the hotels in this town, all night long﻿ ﻿… It’s as much as my job is worth﻿ ﻿…”
She put her handkerchief to her lips to hide a smile that she knew would be too cruel for him not to notice. And even when she took it away, he said:
“Hang it all, what a cruel-looking fiend you are!﻿ ﻿… Why the devil do I hang around you?﻿ ﻿… There’s a picture that my mother’s got, by Burne-Jones﻿ ﻿… A cruel-looking woman with a distant smile﻿ ﻿… Some vampire﻿ ﻿… La Belle Dame sans Merci﻿ ﻿… That’s what you’re like.”
She looked at him suddenly with considerable seriousness﻿ ﻿…
“See here, Potty﻿ ﻿…” she began. He groaned:
“I believe you’d like me to be sent to the beastly trenches﻿ ﻿… Yet a big, distinguished-looking chap like me wouldn’t have a chance﻿ ﻿… At the first volley the Germans fired, they’d pick me off﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh, Potty,” she exclaimed, “try to be serious for a minute﻿ ﻿… I tell you I’m a woman who’s trying﻿ ﻿… who’s desperately wanting﻿ ﻿… to be reconciled to her husband!﻿ ﻿… I would not tell that to another soul﻿ ﻿… I would not tell it to myself﻿ ﻿… But one owes something﻿ ﻿… a parting scene, if nothing else﻿ ﻿… Well, something﻿ ﻿… to a man one’s been in bed with﻿ ﻿… I didn’t give you a parting scene at﻿ ﻿… ah, Yssingueux-les-Pervenches﻿ ﻿… so I give you this tip instead﻿ ﻿…”
He said:
“Will you leave your bedroom door unlocked, or won’t you?”
She said:
“If that man would throw his handkerchief to me, I would follow him round the world in my shift!﻿ ﻿… Look here﻿ ﻿… see me shake when I think of it﻿ ﻿…” She held out her hand at the end of her long arm: hand and arm trembled together, minutely, then very much﻿ ﻿… “Well,” she finished, “if you see that and still want to come to my room﻿ ﻿… your blood be on your own head﻿ ﻿…” She paused for a breath or two and then said:
“You can come﻿ ﻿… I won’t lock my door﻿ ﻿… But I don’t say that you’ll get anything﻿ ﻿… or that you’ll like what you get﻿ ﻿… That’s a fair tip﻿ ﻿…” She added suddenly: “You *sale fat*﻿ ﻿… take what you get and be damned to you!”
Major Perowne had suddenly taken to twirling his moustaches; he said:
“Oh, I’ll chance the A.P.M.s﻿ ﻿…”
She suddenly coiled her legs into her chair.
“I know now what I came here for,” she said.
---
Major Wilfrid Fosbrooke Eddicker Perowne of Perowne, the son of his mother, was one of those individuals who have no history, no strong proclivities, nothing. His knowledge seemed to be bounded by the contents of his newspaper for the immediate day; at any rate, his conversation never went any farther. He was not bold, he was not shy; he was neither markedly courageous nor markedly cowardly. His mother was immoderately wealthy, owned an immense castle that hung over crags, above a western sea, much as a birdcage hangs from a window of a high tenement building, but she received few or no visitors, her cuisine being indifferent and her wine atrocious. She had strong temperance opinions and, immediately after the death of her husband, she had emptied the contents of his cellar, which were almost as historic as his castle, into the sea, a shudder going through county-family England. But even this was not enough to make Perowne himself notorious.
His mother allowed him﻿—after an eye-opener in early youth﻿—the income of a junior royalty, but he did nothing with it. He lived in a great house in Palace Gardens, Kensington, and he lived all alone with rather a large staff of servants who had been selected by his mother, but they did nothing at all, for he ate all his meals, and even took his bath and dressed for dinner at the Bath Club. He was otherwise parsimonious.
He had, after the fashion of his day, passed a year or two in the army when young. He had been first gazetted to His Majesty’s Forty-second Regiment, but on the Black Watch proceeding to India he had exchanged into the Glamorganshires, at that time commanded by General Campion and recruiting in and around Lincolnshire. The general had been an old friend of Perowne’s mother, and, on being promoted to brigadier, had taken Perowne on to his staff as his galloper, for, although Perowne rode rather indifferently, he had a certain social knowledge and could be counted on to know how correctly to address a regimental invitation to a dowager countess who had married a viscount’s third son﻿ ﻿… As a military figure otherwise he had a very indifferent word of command, a very poor drill and next to no control of his men, but he was popular with his batmen, and in a rather stiff way was presentable in the old scarlet uniform or the blue mess jacket. He was exactly six-foot, to a hairbreadth, in his stockings, had very dark eyes, and a rather grating voice; the fact that his limbs were a shade too bulky for his trunk, which was not at all corpulent, made him appear a little clumsy. If in a club you asked what sort of a fellow he was your interlocutor would tell you, most probably, that he had or was supposed to have warts on his head, this to account for his hair which all his life he had combed back, unparted from his forehead. But as a matter of fact he had no warts on his head.
He had once started out on an expedition to shoot big game in Portuguese East Africa. But on its arrival his expedition was met with the news that the natives of the interior were in revolt, so Perowne had returned to Kensington Palace Gardens. He had had several mild successes with women, but, owing to his habits of economy and fear of imbroglios, until the age of thirty-four, he had limited the field of his amours to young women of the lower social orders﻿ ﻿…
His affair with Sylvia Tietjens might have been something to boast about, but he was not boastful, and indeed he had been too hard hit when she had left him even to bear to account lyingly for the employment of the time he had spent with her in Brittany. Fortunately no one took sufficient interest in his movements to wait for his answer to their indifferent questions as to where he had spent the summer. When his mind reverted to her desertion of him moisture would come out of his eyes, undemonstratively, as water leaves the surface of a sponge﻿ ﻿…
Sylvia had left him by the simple expedient of stepping without so much as a reticule on to the little French tramway that took you to the main railway line. From there she had written to him in pencil on a closed correspondence card that she had left him because she simply could not bear either his dullness or his craking voice. She said they would probably run up against each other in the course of the autumn season in town and, after purchase of some night things, had made straight for the German spa to which her mother had retreated.
At the later date Sylvia had no difficulty in accounting to herself for her having gone off with such an oaf: she had simply reacted in a violent fit of sexual hatred, from her husband’s mind. And she could not have found a mind more utterly dissimilar than Perowne’s in any decently groomed man to be found in London. She could recall, even in the French hotel lounge, years after, the almost painful emotion of joyful hatred that had visited her when she had first thought of going off with him. It was the self-applause of one who has just hit upon an excruciatingly inspiring intellectual discovery. In her previous transitory infidelities to Christopher she had discovered that, however presentable the man with whom she might have been having an affair, and however short the affair, even if it were only a matter of a weekend﻿ ﻿… Christopher had spoilt her for the other man. It was the most damnable of his qualities that to hear any other man talk of any subject﻿—any, any subject﻿—from stable form to the balance of power, or from the voice of a given opera singer to the recurrence of a comet﻿—to have to pass a weekend with any other man and hear his talk after having spent the inside of the week with Christopher, hate his ideas how you might, was the difference between listening to a grown man and, with an intense boredom, trying to entertain an inarticulate schoolboy. As beside him, other men simply did not seem ever to have grown up﻿ ﻿…
Just before, with an extreme suddenness, consenting to go away with Perowne, the illuminating idea had struck her: If I did go away with him it would be the most humiliating thing I could do to Christopher﻿ ﻿… And just when the idea *had* struck her, beside her chair in the conservatory at a dance given by the general’s sister, Lady Claudine Sandbach, Perowne, his voice rendered more throaty and less disagreeable than usual by emotion, had been going on and on begging her to elope with him﻿ ﻿… She had suddenly said:
“Very well﻿ ﻿… let’s﻿ ﻿…”
His emotion had been so unbridled in its astonishment that she had, even at that, almost been inclined to treat her own speech as a joke and to give up the revenge﻿ ﻿… But the idea of the humiliation that Christopher must feel proved too much for her. For, for your wife to throw you over for an attractive man is naturally humiliating, but that she should leave you publicly for a man of hardly any intelligence at all, you priding yourself on your brains, must be nearly as mortifying a thing as can happen to you.
But she had hardly set out upon her escapade before two very serious defects in her plan occurred to her with extreme force: the one that, however humiliated Christopher might feel, she would not be with him to witness his humiliation; the other that, oaf as she had taken Perowne to be in casual society, in close daily relationship he was such an oaf as to be almost insufferable. She had imagined that he would prove a person out of whom it might be possible to make *something* by a judicious course of alternated mothering and scorn: she discovered that his mother had already done for him almost all that woman could do. For, when he had been an already rather backward boy at a private school, his mother had kept him so extremely short of pocket-money that he had robbed other boys’ desks of a few shillings here and there﻿—in order to subscribe towards a birthday present for the headmaster’s wife. His mother, to give him a salutary lesson, had given so much publicity to the affair that he had become afflicted with a permanent bent towards shyness that rendered him by turns very mistrustful of himself or very boastful and, although he repressed manifestations of either tendency towards the outside world, the continual repression rendered him almost incapable of any vigorous thought or action﻿ ﻿…
That discovery did not soften Sylvia towards him: it was, as she expressed it, *his* funeral and, although she would have been ready for any normal job of smartening up a roughish man, she was by no means prepared to readjust other women’s hopeless maternal misfits.
So she had got no farther than Ostend, where they had proposed to spend a week or so at the tables, before she found herself explaining to some acquaintances whom she met that she was in that gay city merely for an hour or two, between trains, on the way to join her mother in a German health resort. The impulse to say that had come upon her by surprise, for, until that moment, being completely indifferent to criticism, she had intended to cast no veil at all over her proceedings. But, quite suddenly, on seeing some well-known English faces in the casino it had come over her to think that, however much she imagined Christopher to be humiliated by her going off with an oaf like Perowne, that humiliation must be as nothing compared with that which she might be expected to feel at having found no one better than an oaf like Perowne to go off with. Moreover﻿ ﻿… she began to miss Christopher.
These feelings did not grow any less intense in the rather stuffy but inconspicuous hotel in the Rue St. Roque in Paris to which she immediately transported the bewildered but uncomplaining Perowne, who had imagined that he was to be taken to Wiesbaden for a course of light gaieties. And Paris, when you avoid the more conspicuous resorts, and when you are unprovided with congenial companionship, can prove nearly as overwhelming as is, say, Birmingham on a Sunday.
So that Sylvia waited for only just long enough to convince herself that her husband had no apparent intention of applying for an immediate divorce and had, indeed, no apparent intention of doing anything at all. She sent him, that is to say, a postcard saying that all letters and other communications would reach her at her inconspicuous hotel﻿—and it mortified her not a little to have to reveal the fact that her hotel was so inconspicuous. But, except that her own correspondence was forwarded to her with regularity, no communications at all came from Tietjens.
In an air-resort in the centre of France to which she next removed Perowne, she found herself considering rather seriously what it might be expected that Tietjens *would* do. Through indirect and unsuspecting allusions in letters from her personal friends she found that if Tietjens did not put up, he certainly did not deny, the story that she had gone to nurse or be with her mother, who was supposed to be seriously ill﻿ ﻿… That is to say, her friends said how rotten it was that her mother, Mrs. Satterthwaite, should be so seriously ill; how rotten it must be for her to be shut up in a potty little German *kur-ort* when the world could be so otherwise amusing: and how well Christopher whom they saw from time to time seemed to be getting on considering how rotten it must be for him to be left all alone﻿ ﻿…
At about this time Perowne began to become, if possible, more irritating than ever. In their air-resort, although the guests were almost entirely French, there was a newly opened golf-course, and at the game of golf Perowne displayed an inefficiency and at the same time a morbid conceit that were surprising in one naturally lymphatic. He would sulk for a whole evening if either Sylvia or any Frenchman beat him in a round, and, though Sylvia was by then completely indifferent to his sulking, what was very much worse was that he became gloomily and loud-voicedly quarrelsome over his games with foreign opponents.
Three events, falling within ten minutes of each other, made her determined to get as far away from that air-resort as was feasible. In the first place she observed at the end of the street some English people called Thurston, whose faces she faintly knew, and the emotion she suddenly felt let her know how extremely anxious she was that she should let it remain feasible for Tietjens to take her back. Then, in the golf clubhouse, to which she found herself fiercely hurrying in order to pay her bill and get her clubs, she overheard the conversation of two players that left no doubt in her mind that Perowne had been detected in little meannesses of moving his ball at golf or juggling with his score﻿ ﻿… This was almost more than she could stand. And, at the same moment, her mind, as it were, condescended to let her remember Christopher’s voice as it had once uttered the haughty opinion that no man one could speak to would ever think of divorcing any woman. If he could not defend the sanctity of his hearth he must lump it unless the woman wanted to divorce him﻿ ﻿…
At the time when he had said it her mind﻿—she had been just then hating him a good deal﻿—had seemed to take no notice of the utterance. But now that it presented itself forcibly to her again it brought with it the thought: Supposing he wasn’t really only talking through his hat!﻿ ﻿… She dragged the wretched Perowne off his bed where he had been lost in an after-lunch slumber and told him that they must both leave that place at once, and, that as soon as they reached Paris or some larger town where he could find waiters and people to understand his French, she herself was going to leave him for good. They did not, in consequence, get away from the air-resort until the six o’clock train next morning. Perowne’s passion of rage and despair at the news that she wished to leave him took an inconvenient form, for instead of announcing any intention of committing suicide, as might have been expected, he became gloomily and fantastically murderous. He said that unless Sylvia swore on a little relic of St. Anthony she carried that she had no intention of leaving him he would incontinently kill her. He said, as he said for the rest of his days, that she had ruined his life and caused great moral deterioration in himself. But for her he might have married some pure young thing. Moreover, influencing him against his mother’s doctrines, she had forced him to drink wine, by an effect of pure scorn. Thus he had done harm, he was convinced, both to his health and to his manly proportions﻿ ﻿… It was indeed for Sylvia one of the most unbearable things about this man﻿—the way he took wine. With every glass he put to his lips he would exclaim with an unbearable titter some such imbecility as: “Here is another nail in my coffin.” And he had taken to wine, and even to stronger liquor, very well.
Sylvia had refused to swear by St. Anthony. She definitely was not going to introduce the saint into her amorous affairs, and she definitely was not going to take on any relic an oath that she meant to break at an early opportunity. There was such a thing as playing it too low down: there are dishonours to which death is preferable. So, getting hold of his revolver at a time when he was wringing his hands, she dropped it into the water-jug and then felt reasonably safe.
Perowne knew no French and next to nothing about France, but he had discovered that the French did nothing to you for killing a woman who intended to leave you. Sylvia, on the other hand, was pretty certain that, without a weapon, he could not do much to her. If she had had no other training at her very expensive school she had had so much drilling in calisthenics as to be singularly mistress of her limbs, and, in the interests of her beauty she had always kept herself very fit﻿ ﻿…
She said at last:
“Very well. We will go to Yssingueux-les-Pervenches﻿ ﻿…”
A rather pleasant French couple in the hotel had spoken of this little place in the extreme west of France as a lonely paradise, they having spent their honeymoon there﻿ ﻿… And Sylvia wanted a lonely paradise if there was going to be any scrapping before she got away from Perowne﻿ ﻿…
She had no hesitation as to what she was going to do: the long journey across half France by miserable trains had caused her an agony of homesickness! Nothing less!﻿ ﻿… It was a humiliating disease from which to suffer. But it was unavoidable, like mumps. You had to put up with it. Besides, she even found herself wanting to see her child, whom she imagined herself to hate, as having been the cause of all her misfortunes﻿ ﻿…
She therefore prepared, after great thought, a letter telling Tietjens that she intended to return to him. She made the letter as nearly as possible like one she would write announcing her return from a country house to which she should have been invited for an indefinite period, and she added some rather hard instructions about her maid, these being intended to remove from the letter any possible trace of emotion. She was certain that, if she showed any emotion at all, Christopher would never take her under his roof again﻿ ﻿… She was pretty certain that no gossip had been caused by her escapade. Major Thurston had been at the railway station when they had left, but they had not spoken﻿—and Thurston was a very decentish, brown-moustached fellow, of the sort that does not gossip.
It had proved a little difficult to get away, for Perowne during several weeks watched her like an attendant in a lunatic asylum. But at last the idea presented itself to him that she would never go without her frocks, and, one day, in a fit of intense somnolence after a lunch, washed down with rather a large quantity of the local and fiery cordial, he let her take a walk alone﻿ ﻿…
---
She was by that time tired of men﻿ ﻿… or she imagined that she was; for she was not prepared to be certain, considering the muckers she saw women coming all round her over the most unpresentable individuals. Men, at any rate never fulfilled expectations. They might, upon acquaintance, turn out more entertaining than they appeared; but almost always taking up with a man was like reading a book you had read when you had forgotten that you had read it. You had not been for ten minutes in any sort of intimacy with any man before you said: “But I’ve read all this before﻿ ﻿…” You knew the opening, you were already bored by the middle, and, especially, you knew the end﻿ ﻿…
She remembered, years ago, trying to shock her mother’s spiritual adviser, Father Consett, whom they had lately murdered in Ireland, along with Casement﻿ ﻿… The poor saint had not in the least been shocked. He had gone her one better. For when she had said something like that her idea of a divvy life﻿—they used in those days to say “divvy”﻿—would be to go off with a different man every weekend, he had told her that after a short time she would be bored already by the time the poor dear fellow was buying the railway ticket﻿ ﻿…
And, by heavens, he had been right﻿ ﻿… For when she came to think of it, from the day that poor saint had said that thing in her mother’s sitting-room in the little German spa﻿—Lobscheid, it must have been called﻿—in the candlelight, his shadow denouncing her from all over the walls, to now when she sat in the palmish brickwork of that hotel that had been new-whitely decorated to celebrate hostilities, never once had she sat in a train with a man who had any right to look upon himself as justified in mauling her about﻿ ﻿… She wondered if, from where he sat in heaven, Father Consett would be satisfied with her as he looked down into that lounge﻿ ﻿… Perhaps it was really he that had pulled off that change in her﻿ ﻿…
Never once till yesterday﻿ ﻿… For perhaps the unfortunate Perowne might just faintly have had the right yesterday to make himself for about two minutes﻿—before she froze him into a choking, pallid snowman with goggle eyes﻿—the perfectly loathsome thing that a man in a railway train becomes﻿ ﻿… Much too bold and yet stupidly awkward with the fear of the guard looking in at the window, the train doing over sixty, without corridors﻿ ﻿… No, never again for *me*, father, she addressed her voice towards the ceiling﻿ ﻿…
Why in the world couldn’t you get a man to go away with you and be just﻿—oh, light comedy﻿—for a whole, a whole blessed weekend. For a whole blessed life﻿ ﻿… Why not?﻿ ﻿… Think of it﻿ ﻿… A whole blessed life with a good sort and yet didn’t go all gurgly in the voice, and codfish-eyed and all-overish﻿—to the extent of not being able to find the tickets when asked for them﻿ ﻿… Father, dear, she said again upwards, if I could find men like that, that would be just heaven﻿ ﻿… where there is no marrying﻿ ﻿… But, of course, she went on almost resignedly, he would not be faithful to you﻿ ﻿… And then: one would have to stand it﻿ ﻿…
She sat up so suddenly in her chair that beside her, too, Major Perowne nearly jumped out of his wickerwork, and asked if *he* had come back﻿ ﻿… She exclaimed:
“No, I’d be damned if I would﻿ ﻿… I’d be damned, I’d be damned, I’d be damned if I would﻿ ﻿… Never. Never. By the living God!”
She asked fiercely of the agitated major:
“Has Christopher got a girl in this town?﻿ ﻿… You’d better tell me the truth!”
The major mumbled:
“He﻿ ﻿… No﻿ ﻿… He’s too much of a stick﻿ ﻿… He never even goes to Suzette’s﻿ ﻿… Except once to fetch out some miserable little squit of a subaltern who was smashing up Mother Hardelot’s furniture﻿ ﻿…”
He grumbled:
“But you shouldn’t give a man the jumps like that!﻿ ﻿… Be conciliatory, you said﻿ ﻿…” He went on to grumble that her manners had not improved since she had been at Yssingueux-les-Pervenches﻿ ﻿… and then went on to tell her that in French the words *yeux des pervenches* meant eyes of periwinkle blue. And that was the only French he knew, because a Frenchman he had met in the train had told him so and he had always thought that if *her* eyes had been periwinkle blue﻿ ﻿… “But you’re not listening﻿ ﻿… Hardly polite, I call it,” he had mumbled to a conclusion﻿ ﻿…
She was sitting forward in her chair still clenching her hand under her chin at the thought that perhaps Christopher had Valentine Wannop in that town. That was perhaps why he elected to remain there. She asked:
“Why does Christopher stay on in this Godforsaken hole?﻿ ﻿… The inglorious base, they call it﻿ ﻿…”
“Because he’s jolly well got to﻿ ﻿…” Major Perowne said. “He’s got to do what he’s told﻿ ﻿…”
She said: “Christopher!﻿ ﻿… You mean to say they’d keep a man like *Christopher* anywhere he didn’t want to be﻿ ﻿…”
“They’d jolly well knock spots off him if he went away,” Major Perowne exclaimed﻿ ﻿… “What the deuce do you think your blessed fellow is?﻿ ﻿… The King of England?﻿ ﻿…” He added with a sudden sombre ferocity: “They’d shoot him like anybody else if he bolted﻿ ﻿… What do *you* think?”
She said: “But all that wouldn’t prevent his having a girl in this town?”
“Well, he hasn’t got one,” Perowne said. “He sticks up in that blessed old camp of his like a blessed she-chicken sitting on addled eggs﻿ ﻿… That’s what they say of him﻿ ﻿… I don’t know anything about the fellow﻿ ﻿…”
Listening vindictively and indolently, she thought she caught in his droning tones a touch of the homicidal lunacy that had used to underlie his voice in the bedroom at Yssingueux. The fellow had undoubtedly about him a touch of the dull, mad murderer of the police-courts. With a sudden animation she thought:
“Suppose he tried to murder Christopher﻿ ﻿…” And she imagined her husband breaking the fellow’s back across his knee, the idea going across her mind as fire traverses the opal. Then, with a dry throat, she said to herself:
“I’ve got to find out whether he has that girl in Rouen﻿ ﻿…” Men stuck together. The fellow Perowne might well be protecting Tietjens. It would be unthinkable that any rules of the service could keep Christopher in that place. They could not shut up the upper classes. If Perowne had any sense he would know that to shield Tietjens was the way not to get her﻿ ﻿… But he had no sense﻿ ﻿… Besides, sexual solidarity was a terribly strong thing﻿ ﻿… She knew that she herself would not give a woman’s secrets away in order to get her man. Then﻿ ﻿… how was she to ascertain whether the girl was not in that town? How?﻿ ﻿… She imagined Tietjens going home every night to her﻿ ﻿… But he was going to spend that night with herself﻿ ﻿… She knew that﻿ ﻿… Under that roof﻿ ﻿… Fresh from the other﻿ ﻿…
She imagined him there, now﻿ ﻿… In the parlour of one of the little villas you see from the tram on the top of the town﻿ ﻿… They were undoubtedly, now, discussing her﻿ ﻿… Her whole body writhed, muscle on muscle, in her chair﻿ ﻿… She must discover﻿ ﻿… But how do you discover? Against a universal conspiracy﻿ ﻿… This whole war was an agapemone﻿ ﻿… You went to war when you desired to rape innumerable women﻿ ﻿… It was what war was for﻿ ﻿… All these men, crowded in this narrow space﻿ ﻿… She stood up:
“I’m going,” she said, “to put on a little powder for Lady Sachse’s feast﻿ ﻿… You needn’t stay if you don’t want to﻿ ﻿…” She was going to watch every face she saw until it gave up the secret of where in that town Christopher had the Wannop girl hidden﻿ ﻿… She imagined her freckled, snubnosed faced pressed﻿—squashed was the word﻿—against his cheek﻿ ﻿… She was going to investigate﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

在那座城里头等旅馆的休憩厅里——陈设精美考究，白瓷漆的面饰，藤编的家具，四壁皆镜——西尔维娅·蒂金斯（Sylvia Tietjens）坐在一把藤椅上，心不在焉地似听非听，一位参谋少校正哭腔哭调、絮絮不休地央求她今夜别锁上卧室的门。她说：
“我不知道……是啊，也许吧……我不知道……”说着，目光漠然投向一面泛蓝的壁镜，那镜子同其余所有的镜面一样，镶着漆成白色的软木框。她微微一挺身，说道：
“克里斯托弗（Christopher）来了！”
参谋少校的帽子、手杖和手套全都掉了。他那头黑发不分头路、抹着厚厚一层黏糊糊的发蜡，此刻在头皮上慌乱地颤动。他方才一直在说西尔维娅毁了他的一生。西尔维娅难道不知道她毁了他的一生？若不是她，他本可以娶个纯洁的年轻姑娘。这会儿他喊道：
“可他想干什么？……老天爷！……他到底想干什么？”
“他想，”西尔维娅说，“演一出耶稣基督。”
佩罗恩（Perowne）少校叫了起来：
“耶稣基督！……可他是将军麾下嘴最脏的军官……”
“哦，”西尔维娅说，“你若真娶了你那纯洁的年轻姑娘，不出九个月她就给你……那词儿怎么说来着？……戴上绿帽子了……”
佩罗恩听到这个词儿打了个哆嗦。他咕哝道：
“我不明白……好像倒过来了……”
“哦，倒不了，”西尔维娅说，“好好想想吧……从道义上讲，你才是做丈夫的……不道德地讲——我该这么说……因为他是我想要的男人……他气色不好……医院当局总会告诉做妻子的，她们的丈夫到底得了什么病吗？”
从他那半欠起身子的椅子的角度望过去，在他眼里西尔维娅像是正对着一堵空墙瞧。“我看不到他。”佩罗恩说。
“我在镜子里能看见他，”西尔维娅说，“瞧！从这儿就能看见他。”
佩罗恩又哆嗦了一下。
“我不想看见他……公务在身，有时候免不了要见他……我可不喜欢……”
西尔维娅说：
“就凭你，”那口气轻蔑至极，“你只会给黄毛丫头们送巧克力……公务上他怎么会撞见你？……你根本不算个军人！”
佩罗恩说：
“可咱们怎么办？他会干什么？”
“我嘛，”西尔维娅答道，“等小听差拿着他的名片来，就吩咐说我有客……他会干什么，我不知道。多半会揍你……他这会儿正瞧着你的后背呢……”
佩罗恩僵住了，整个人陷进深椅子里。
“可他总不至于吧！”他激动地喊道，“你方才还说他在演耶稣基督。我主可不会在旅馆休憩厅里动手打人……”
“我主！”西尔维娅轻蔑地说，“你知道什么我主？……我主是位绅士……克里斯托弗这是在学我主探望那行淫时被拿的妇人……他这是在向我提供社交上的体面，在他看来，做我丈夫的本分就该如此。”
一位独臂蓄须的侍者领班，穿过一组组供人密谈的扶手椅，朝他们走来。他说：
“对不起……我起先没瞧见夫人……”说着在托盘上呈上一张名片。西尔维娅看也不看便说：
“去对那位先生说……说我没空。”领班矜持地退开了。
“可他会把我撕碎的……”佩罗恩喊道，“我该怎么办？……我究竟该怎么办？”他要找条退路，除非从蒂特金斯（Tietjens）的脸上跨过去。
西尔维娅脊背笔挺，带着毒蛇盯住小鸟的神情，直望前方，一言不发，末了忽然叫道：
“看在上帝份上，别再发抖了……他不会把你怎么样的，你这样的丫头片子……他是个男人……”佩罗恩那把藤椅一直喳喳地响个不停，跟在火车车厢里似的。这声响猛地一下停了……她忽然攥紧双手，从齿缝里迸出一口怨毒的气。
“凭不朽的众圣徒起誓，”她喊道，“我发誓总有一天要叫他那张木头脸露出痛色。”
---
几分钟前，在那面泛蓝的镜子里，她看见了丈夫那双玛瑙蓝的眼睛，隔着三十英尺，越过一排排扶手椅，从棕榈扇叶的缝隙间望过来。他站在那儿，手里握一根马鞭，那身不合体的军装穿在身上显得相当笨拙。相当笨拙，而且疲惫不堪，却全无表情！他直直望进镜子中她那双眼睛的映像，随即又移开目光。他侧过身去，只把侧脸对着她，继续一动不动地凝视着一颗麅鹿头标本，那下面的墙面正对着通向旅馆内部的玻璃门。旅馆侍者向他走去时，他掏出一张名片交给侍者，说了三个词。她看见他的嘴唇动了三下：克里斯托弗·蒂特金斯太太。她低声说：
“该死的骑士风度！……哦，上帝诅咒他这骑士风度！”她知道他心里在想什么。他看见她同佩罗恩在一起，所以既不朝她走来，也不指给侍者她坐的地方。怕她难堪！去不去见，他留给她的意愿。
镜子里看得见那侍者七拐八绕地去了又回，蒂特金斯仍旧凝视着那颗麅鹿头。他接过名片，放回皮夹，然后对侍者说了句什么。侍者以一种他那个阶层惯有的客套耸了耸肩，肩膀还端着，一只手指着里门，领先走进旅馆，蒂特金斯跟在后面。接回名片时，蒂特金斯脸上没有一丝纹路动过。就在那时，西尔维娅发誓，她总有一天要叫他那张木头脸露出痛色……
他那张脸叫人无法忍受。沉重；纹丝不动。不是倨傲，只是望尽万物众生的头顶之上，望进一个他们谁也进不去的、太遥远的世界……可他又那么笨拙、那么疲惫，在她看来，迫害这样一个人几乎算不得光彩。就像鞭打一条垂死的斗牛犬……
她几乎带着点灰心，颓然靠回椅背。她说：
“他进旅馆里去了……”
佩罗恩在椅子里激动地往前一探。他嚷嚷着要走。随即又灰心丧气地陷了回去：
“不，我不走了，”他说，“我留在这儿多半更安全。出去没准儿会撞上他。”
“你算是明白过来了，我的裙子能保你平安，”西尔维娅轻蔑地说，“当然，克里斯托弗当着我的面绝不会动手打人。”
佩罗恩少校打断她，问道：
“他想干什么？他在旅馆里做什么？”蒂特金斯太太说：
“你猜！”她又说，“换了你处在同样的境地，你会怎么做？”
“跑去砸烂你的卧室，”佩罗恩应声便答，“我发现你离开伊桑格（Yssingueux）的时候，我干的就是这个。”
西尔维娅说：
“啊，那地方原来是叫这个名字。”
佩罗恩呻吟道：
“你无心无肺，”他说，“没有别的词好形容。无心无肺。你就是这么个人。”
西尔维娅心不在焉地问他，为什么偏偏在这个节骨眼上说她无心无肺。她正想象着克里斯托弗笨重地踢踏着脚步，沿旅馆走廊一间间看卧室，然后塞给侍者一笔可观的小费，好确保被安排在她同一层楼。她几乎听得见他那并不讨厌的男人嗓音，带着胸腔里透出的微颤，也令她颤动。
佩罗恩还在嘟囔。说西尔维娅无心无肺，因为她竟忘掉了那座布列塔尼小村的名字——他们在那里共度过三个星期的极乐时光；她走得那么仓促，随身衣物全留在了旅馆里。
“得了，那对我可算不上什么乐子。”等她再搭理他时，西尔维娅接着说，“老天爷！……你以为跟你在一块儿，pour tout potage（就算白饶），能有什么乐子？我凭什么要记住那个可恶地方的名字？”
佩罗恩说：
“伊桑格-莱佩尔旺什，多好听的名字，”话里带着责怪。
“没用的，”西尔维娅答道，“想在我心里唤起多愁善感的回忆，你死了这条心吧。你要想跟我继续好下去，就得让我忘了你那副德行……我坐在这儿听你那秧鸡似的嗓音，是要等克里斯托弗出旅馆去……然后我回房梳妆，准备萨克塞夫人的宴会，你就坐在这儿等我。”
“我才不去，”佩罗恩说，“萨克塞夫人的宴会。哼，他可是婚约签署仪式的主要见证人之一，老坎皮恩（Campion）和参谋部那班人也都到场……你可逮不着我……我自有‘突然另有要约’这一手。没门儿。”
“你会陪我去的，小男人，”西尔维娅说，“要是你还想再沐浴我的微笑……我可不独自去赴萨克塞夫人的宴会，在半个法国贵族院的众目睽睽之下，活像连个护送的男人都抓不着……要是他们真有贵族院的话！……你可逮不着我……没门儿！”她学着他那副公鸭嗓，“你只消露个脸儿，充当我的护送人，过后就可以走……”
“可是，老天爷！”佩罗恩嚷道，“那正是我万万干不得的事。坎皮恩说了，要是再听说我跟你招摇过市，就把我打发回我那该死的团。我那该死的团可正在战壕里……你可别想看见我进战壕，对不对？”
“我倒宁可看你在战壕里，也不想看你在我的房里，”西尔维娅说，“哪一天都一样！”
“啊，可不是嘛！”佩罗恩顿时来了精神，“我怎么担保照你的意思办了，就能沐浴你所谓的微笑？我带你来这儿，没带证件，惹出了天大的麻烦。你从没说过你没有证件。宪兵司令奥哈拉（O’Hara）将军为这事大发雷霆……可我落着什么好了？……连个笑影儿都没有……你真该瞧瞧老奥哈拉那张紫红的脸！……有人把他从午觉里叫醒，向他报告你这桩十恶不赦的案子，他到现在还没缓过那口消化不良……再说，他恨蒂特金斯……蒂特金斯总跟他那帮宪兵过不去……奥哈拉的心肝羔羊……”
西尔维娅没在听，却对着心里的一念慢慢微笑。这可把他气疯了。
“你打的什么主意？”他喊道，“见鬼，你打的什么主意？……你总不会是来这儿看……他的。照我看，你也不是来看我的。那么……”
西尔维娅睁大双眼回过头来看他，仿佛刚从沉睡中醒来。
“我来连我自己都不知道，”她说，“突然起了念头要来。动身前十分钟起的念头。我就来了。我不知道要证件。我要是想要，想必是弄得着的……你从没问过我有没有证件。你就是死缠住我，把我拽进了你的专车……我也不知道你会来。”
这在佩罗恩听来简直是侮辱到了头。他喊道：
“哦，岂有此理，西尔维娅！你一定知道……周三晚上你在奎克家的聚会上。他们都知道。我最要好的朋友们。”
“是你自讨的，”她说，“我不知道……我要是知道你坐那班车，我就不会坐那班车来。是你逼得我对你出言不逊。”她又添一句：“你就不能迁就点儿？”好让他安静一会儿。他的下巴耷拉下来。
她心里正琢磨，克里斯托弗是哪里来的钱付旅馆的床位。就在不久前，她把他银行账户里的余额提得一干二净，只留了一先令。现在是月中，他不可能再支饷银……那当然是她的试探。或许能逼得他抗议几句。同样，她也试探过，指控他卷走了她的床单。那纯属任意胡为，等她再看看他那张纹丝不动的脸，她明白自己干得相当蠢……可她已是山穷水尽：从前她试过对丈夫横加指控，却从没试过给他制造不便……此刻她忽然醒悟到自己蠢到了什么地步。他心里雪亮，她那些小打小闹的凶悍根本不是她的本色；所以他同样心里雪亮，每一件都不过是试探。他会说：“她想逼我嗷嗷叫。我偏不！”
她得采取厉害得多的手段。她说：“他得服帖……他得服帖……他非服帖不可。”
佩罗恩少校这时合上了下巴。他在寻思。有一回咕哝道：“迁就点儿！我的老天！”
她的情绪忽然高涨起来：是看见克里斯托弗闹的：确凿无疑，他们又要住在同一个屋檐底下了。他决不会跟万诺普（Wannop）家那丫头好上——这她敢拿全部家当加不朽的灵魂打赌。而且这赌注稳赢！……只是战争结束以后他们之间会是什么关系，她心里没数。起初她以为，那天凌晨四点从他们的公寓出走，就是一拍两散了。看起来顺理成章。可是渐渐地，退守伯肯黑德，在那间修女般洁白安静的屋子里，疑虑爬上她心头。像他们那样过日子，坏处之一就是心里的想法很少说出口。可这有时也是好处。她本来确实是存心一别到底的。她向出租汽车司机报站名时，确实有意提高了嗓门，心里相当笃定他会听见；她也几乎确信，他会把这当作信号，看作他们这段姻缘的气息已经断了……几乎确信。可就差那么一点！……
她宁死也不肯给他写信；如今她同样宁死不肯露半点口风，说她想要他们再住到同一个屋檐下……她自言自语：
“他是不是在给那丫头写信？”随即又想：“不会！……我确信他没有。”……她在公寓里扣下了他所有的信件，只放几封广告传单漏过去给他，好让他以为信件都照常转来。从她读过的那些写给他的信里，她相当有把握：除了格雷律师学院的公寓，他没有留过别的地址……可是没有瓦伦丁·万诺普的信……万诺普太太来过两封，他哥哥马克两封，斯卡索港一封，团的军官弟兄一两封，还有些公函便条……她心想，倘若真有那丫头的信，她就会把他所有的信都放过去，连那丫头的一并放……而现在，她倒没有十足把握说自己真会那么做了。
镜子里，她看见克里斯托弗迈着僵硬的步子走出旅馆，沿着她身后那条门对门的小径走去……一种异样的欢喜涌上她心头——绝对确信他没有跟万诺普小姐通信。绝对确信……他心里若当真活泛到那个地步，气色就会不一样。她说不出会是什么样。总之不一样……活泛！也许会不自在这会子：也许……餍足……
少校有一阵子一直在诉他的委屈。他说他成天像条哈巴狗似的跟着她转悠，却一无所获。如今又要他迁就。她说她需要个摆出来撑场面的护送男人。好啊，那当护送的也总该得点什么……偏在这当口他又起了头：
“喂……今晚你到底让不让我去你房里？”
她迸出一阵高声大笑。他说：
“岂有此理，这可不是好笑的事！……听着！你不知道我冒的什么险……这座城里各家旅馆的走廊上，宪兵官、副宪兵官、还有临时代理助理副宪兵官，整夜整夜地转悠……我这差事可就悬了……”
她拿手绢掩住嘴唇，遮住一个微笑——她知道那笑太刻毒，他不会看不见。等她放下手绢，他还是说：
“活见鬼，你这女人生就一副刻薄相！……我到底着了什么魔，老围着你转？……我母亲有幅画，伯恩-琼斯画的……一个带着漠然微笑的刻薄女人……什么吸血妖妇……《无情的妖女》……你就是那副模样。”
她忽然相当严肃地看着他……
“听我说，波蒂……”她开了腔。他呻吟一声：
“我看你巴不得把我送到那该死的战壕里去……像我这样仪表堂堂的大块头可没有活路……德国人第一排枪放过来，就会把我撂倒……”
“哦，波蒂，”她喊道，“正经一分钟吧……我告诉你，我是个正努力……正拼命想要……跟丈夫和好的女人！……这话我对谁都不会说……连对我自己都不会说……可人总欠着点儿什么……好歹是一场分手的戏……总之是那么一点儿……给一个同你上过床的男人……在……啊，伊桑格-莱佩尔旺什，我没跟你演分手的戏……所以我拿这个指点补偿你……”
他说：
“你到底锁不锁卧室的门？”
她说：
“那个男人肯朝我丢一块手绢，我就只穿着贴身衬裙跟他走遍天涯！……瞧着……想想这个，看我抖成什么样……”她从长长的手臂尽头伸出手来：手和臂一起颤抖，起初细细的，随即抖得厉害……“好了，”她收尾道，“你瞧见这样还要来我房里……出了事可怨不得别人……”她喘了一两口气，又说：“你可以来……我不锁门……可我不保证你能得着什么……也不保证你喜欢得着的东西……这指点够公道了……”她忽然又补一句：“你这段下流坯……得什么受什么，活该！”
佩罗恩少校忽然捻起他的小胡子来；他说：
“哦，宪兵官嘛……我豁出去碰碰运气……”
她忽然把双腿一盘，缩进椅子里。
“我现在知道我是为什么来的了。”她说。
---
佩罗恩家的威尔弗里德·福斯布鲁克·埃迪克·佩罗恩少校，他母亲的儿子，是那种没有来历、没有强烈的癖好、什么也没有的人物。他的知识似乎不出当天报纸的内容；至少他的谈话从来超不出这个范围。他不鲁莽，也不怯场；说不上格外勇敢，也说不上格外胆小。他母亲富得漫无边际，拥有一座悬在峭壁上的巨大城堡，俯临西边的大海，很像高层公寓窗上挂着的一只鸟笼，可她几乎不接待客人，因为她的厨艺平平，酒又糟糕透顶。她持激烈的禁酒主义见解，丈夫一死，她便把丈夫酒窖里的存酒——几乎同城堡一样有年头——统统倒进了大海，惹得英格兰的郡望之家打了个寒颤。可就连这样，也不足以让佩罗恩本人出什么名。
他母亲——在他少年时让他吃过一堝之后——给他一份堪比小王子的津贴，可他什么也没拿它来干。他住在肯辛顿宫花园街的一所大宅里，独自住着，雇着相当一大班仆人，都是他母亲挑选的，可他们什么事也没有，因为他一日三餐、连洗澡和赴晚宴前的更衣，全在巴斯俱乐部解决。其余方面他吝啬得很。
年轻时，他照当时的风气在军队里混过一两年。最初他被委任到国王陛下的第四十二团，可黑卫团开拔印度时，他调进了格拉摩根郡团，那时该团由坎皮恩将军指挥，正在林肯郡一带招募。将军是佩罗恩母亲的旧交，升准将时便把佩罗恩带进参谋部当传令副官，因为佩罗恩虽然马骑得稀松，却有些社交上的门道，把全团的请帖怎样合乎规矩地写给一位改嫁子爵第三子的老伯爵夫人，这种事包在他身上准没错……至于作为一名军人，他口令喊得含含糊糊，操练稀松，几乎辖不住兵，可他在勤务兵里人缘不错，穿上老式猩红军装或蓝色晚宴军便服，板着一张脸，倒也拿得出手。他穿袜子量身高不多不少恰好六英尺，眼睛乌黑，嗓音有点刺耳；四肢相对躯干略嫌粗大——躯干倒丝毫不臃肿——这使他显得有点笨拙。若在俱乐部里你打听他是怎么样一个人，跟你搭话的多半会说，他脑袋上长着疣子，或者据信长着，为的是解释他那头发——他一辈子都从额头往后梳，不分头路。可事实上他脑袋上并没有疣子。
他曾启程去葡属东非猎大兽。可远征队刚到，就传来内地土人造反的消息，于是佩罗恩又回到了肯辛顿宫花园街。他在女人身上有过几回不大不小的胜利，但由于节俭的习惯和怕惹是非，直到三十四岁，他的风流都只限于下层社会的年轻女人……
他同西尔维娅·蒂金斯这段私情本可以拿去吹嘘，可他不喜吹嘘，况且她甩掉他那一下太狠，狠得他连为在布列塔尼同她消磨的那段时光撒个谎交代一下都提不起精神。所幸没有人对他的行踪有足够兴趣，肯等他回答那些随口问问夏天上哪儿去了的敷衍问题。每当想起她的离弃，他眼里就会渗出潮气，无声无息，像水从海绵表面沁出来……
西尔维娅甩掉他，用的法子简单得很：连只手提袋都没拿，就跨上了那条通往铁路干线的小法式有轨电车。从那里，她用铅笔在一张背面封口的通信卡上给他写道，她离开他，是因为他的乏味和他的破锣嗓子她实在忍无可忍。她说秋季社交季里两人在城里多半还会碰上；随后她买了些夜间用品，便径直投奔她母亲隐居的德国温泉去了。
到了后来，西尔维娅要向自己解释怎么会跟这么一头蠢驴私奔，毫无难处：那不过是她对丈夫的心智发作了一场狂暴的性憎恨，做出的反应。而在伦敦任何一位体面光鲜的男人身上，她都再找不出一颗比佩罗恩的更截然不同的头脑了。多年之后，甚至在法国旅馆的休憩厅里，她还能回味起初起意跟他私奔时，那阵几乎令她痛苦的、快意的恨意。那感觉，就像一个人刚撞上一项痛切而振奋的智识发现，在为自己喝彩。在先前对克里斯托弗一次次短暂的背叛里，她早已发现：无论同她有染的男人多么拿得出手，无论那段情多么短，哪怕只消一个周末……克里斯托弗都把她惯坏了，惯得容不下另一个男人。他这一宗品质最可恶：听任何别的男人谈任何题目——什么题目都行，什么题目都行——从马厩的格局到欧洲均势，从某位歌剧演员的嗓子到彗星的回归——跟克里斯托弗厮守了一星期之后，再不得不同任何别的男人共度一个周末、听他谈话，哪怕你恨透了他的见解，那滋味就如同听一个成年人说话和憋着极度的厌烦去应酬一个不善言辞的男学生之间的差别。同他一比，别的男人简直就像从来没长大过……
就在她极其突然地答应跟佩罗恩出走之前，灵光一闪的念头击中了她：我要是跟这个人走了，那就是我能给克里斯托弗的最难堪的打击……而恰在这念头击中她的当口，在将军的妹妹克劳迪娜·桑德巴赫夫人举办的舞会上，就在她椅子旁边的暖房里，佩罗恩正因情动而嗓音比平日更沙哑、也由此不那么难听地，没完没了地求她跟他私奔……她突然说：
“很好……走吧……”
他那惊讶中狂喜失态的样子，害得她连那一瞬都几乎想把方才的话当句玩笑，放弃这场报复……可一想到克里斯托弗必定感到的羞辱，她就什么都顾不上了。妻子为一个有魅力的男人甩掉你，固然难堪；可她当着众人跟一个几乎全无智力的男人跑掉——而你还以自己的头脑自负——这必定近乎一个人所能遭受的最丢脸的事了。
可她这场风流冒险还没上路，计划的两大严重缺陷便排山倒海地向她显形：其一，无论克里斯托弗感到多么难堪，她都不在他身边，眼看不到他的难堪；其二，佩罗恩在泛泛之交里虽已蠢得可观，朝夕厮守起来，那份蠢简直叫人忍无可忍。她原以为调教得当，恩威并施——一会儿当妈一会儿白眼——好歹能把他捏出个人形：她这才发现，他母亲早把一个女人能做的差不多全做尽了。他本来就是个相当迟钝的男生，在私立学校里，他母亲把零用钱卡得死紧，紧得他不得不东一榔头西一棒子，从别的男生书桌里偷上几先令——为了凑份子给校长太太买生日礼物。他母亲为了给他一个有益的教训，把这事张扬得人人皆知，害他落下了一辈子的怯场病，时而极度自轻，时而大吹大擂，虽说对外他拼命把这两种倾向都压着，可这长年的压制弄得他几乎再不能进行任何有力的思考或行动……
这一发现并没有让西尔维娅对他心软：用她的话说，那是他自作自受；再说，把一个毛糙男人拾掇出个样子，这类正经的活计她随时肯干，可要她去返工别的女人已经做砸了的、无望的母亲功课，她万万不干。
于是她才走到奥斯坦德——他们本打算在那儿的赌桌边消磨一星期左右——就发觉自己正向碰见的几位熟人解释，说她到这座寻欢作乐的城市不过是换车的个把钟头，正要去德国的一处疗养地陪母亲。这么说的冲动来得猝不及防，因为在那之前，她对旁人的非议全然无所谓，本不打算给自己的行踪遮任何面纱。可就在赌场里看见几张熟稔的英国面孔的一霎，她突然想到：无论她想象克里斯托弗被她跟佩罗恩这样的蠢驴私奔羞辱得多深，比起别人料想她该感到的羞辱——挑来挑去只挑中佩罗恩这么头蠢驴——那份羞辱又算得了什么。更何况……她开始想念克里斯托弗了。
这些情绪，到了巴黎圣罗克街那家闷热却不起眼的旅馆里，也未尝稍减——她立刻把满头雾水却毫无怨言的佩罗恩搬到了那里，而他原本以为这一趟是去威斯巴登逍遥一番的。再说巴黎，你若避开那些扎眼的去处，身边又没有投契的伴，那种难堪简直不亚于——比方说——礼拜天的伯明翰。
于是西尔维娅等啊等，只等到自己确信：丈夫显然无意立即申请离婚，而且显然无意采取任何行动。这就是说，她给他寄了一张明信片，说一切信件和音讯都寄到她那家不起眼的旅馆即可——不得不亮出她的旅馆竟如此不起眼这个事实，多少叫她脸上挂不住。可是，除了她自己的信件照常转来之外，蒂特金斯那边杳无一丝音讯。
在接下来安顿佩罗恩的法国中部一处高原疗养地，她开始相当认真地琢磨，人们料想蒂特金斯会怎么做。从闺中好友来信里那些间接的、浑然不觉的只言片语，她发现，即便蒂特金斯没有主动宣扬，他也绝不否认那个说法：说她去护理或陪伴她重病的母亲了……也就是说，她的朋友们说，她母亲萨特思韦特太太病得那么重，真是糟透了；世道这么有意思，她却闷在德国一个屁大的疗养镇里，真够她受的：还有他们时常见到的克里斯托弗，一个人孤零零被撂下，明明该够呛的，看上去倒还过得不错……
大约就在这时，佩罗恩变得——如果还能更气人的话——比从前更气人了。在他们那处高原疗养地，住客虽几乎全是法国人，却新开了一片高尔夫球场，而在高尔夫球上，佩罗恩显露出的无能，连同病态的自负，在天生懒散的人身上真属罕见。一轮球里无论输给西尔维娅还是哪个法国人，他都能怄一晚上；西尔维娅对他的怄气早已全不在乎，更糟得多的是，同外国对手打球，他会阴沉着脸、大着嗓门跟人吵架。
三件事前后脚落在十分钟之内，让她铁了心要离开那处高原疗养地，能走多远走多远。头一件，她在街尾望见几个姓瑟斯顿的英国人，脸熟得很，她心头骤然涌起的那股情绪让她明白，自己多么急切地巴望给蒂特金斯留着一条收回她的后路。接着，她风急火燎赶到高尔夫球会付账取杆，无意间听见两个球员的交谈，叫她再无疑义：佩罗恩在球场上的小动作被抓住了——挪球，或者经济杆数上做手脚……这她几乎忍无可忍。而也就在同一刻，她的头脑仿佛开恩似的，让她记起克里斯托弗的嗓音，记起他有一次发表的那条傲慢的高见：凡是还够得上交谈的人，就决不会想到跟任何女人离婚。他若保不住自家炉灶的圣洁，那就只好受着，除非那女人自己要离他……
他说这话的时候——那阵子她正恨他恨得厉害——她的头脑似乎根本没理会这句话。可如今这句话又强有力地冒出来，还捎来一个念头：倘若这不真的只是他的大言欺世呢！……她把那可怜的佩罗恩从午饭后酣睡的床上拖起来，告诉他说他们俩必须立刻离开这地方，而且一到巴黎或者随便哪座大些的城市——只要那里有侍者之类的人听得懂他那口法语——她自己就要跟他一刀两断。结果，他们第二天早上六点钟的火车才得以离开那处高原疗养地。
听到她要离开他的消息，佩罗恩那一腔狂怒与绝望发作出来的样子很不省事：他没有像人们料想的那样宣告要自杀，反而阴森古怪地动了杀机。他说，西尔维娅若不拿她随身带的那件圣安东尼的圣物起誓，说她无意离开他，他就立刻杀了她。他说——这话他说了一辈子——她毁了他的一生，使他的道德大为败坏。若不是她，他本可以娶个纯洁的年轻姑娘。还有，她违背他母亲的教义影响他，凭着纯粹的轻蔑逼得他喝起了葡萄酒。就这样，他确信自己既伤了身子，又毁了男子汉的体格……在西尔维娅看来，这个男人最叫人受不了的事情之一，的确就是他喝酒的做派。酒杯每沾一次唇，他都要咯咯傻笑着冒出一句蠢话，比如：“这又是往我棺材上钉的一颗钉子。”而他对酒——连更烈的酒——倒是很上道。
西尔维娅拒绝以圣安东尼起誓。她断乎不要把圣徒牵进她的风流案里，也断乎不肯对着圣物起一个她打算尽早践踏的誓。世上总有些手段卑劣到做不得：有些耻辱，死了都比沾上强。于是，趁他绞着双手的当口，她拿到他的左轮手枪，扔进了水壶，然后觉得多少安全了。
佩罗恩不懂法语，对法国也几乎一无所知，可他打听出来了：杀了存心要离开你的女人，法国人不会把你怎么样。西尔维娅呢，则相当有把握：没了凶器，他奈何她不得。哪怕她在那所学费昂贵得吓人的学校里别的没学到，健身体操是操练足了，四肢听她使唤极了；而且为了她的美貌，她一直把身子保持得极好……
她终于说：
“好吧。我们就去伊桑格-莱佩尔旺什……”
旅馆里一对颇讨人喜欢的法国夫妇说起过这座远在法国西陲的小地方，说它是一座寂寞的天堂，他们是在那儿度的蜜月……西尔维娅要的正是这样一座寂寞的天堂——如果她脱身之前免不了一场厮闹的话……
她对自己要做什么毫不迟疑：坐着一趟趟破火车横穿半个法国的长途，害她害了一场剧烈的思乡病！不折不扣的思乡病！……害这种病，够丢人的。可它没处躲，就跟腮腺炎一样。只好由它。再说，她甚至发觉自己想见孩子了——那个她自以为恨的孩子，恨他是她一切不幸的祸根……
于是她煞费苦心拟了一封信，告诉蒂特金斯她打算回到他身边去。她把信写得尽可能像一封寻常的信——从一座应邀小住、住期无定的乡间宅子回家的那种信——又添上几条关于她贴身女仆的颇为苛刻的吩咐，好把信里任何一点情感的痕迹都刮干净。她确信，只要露出一丝情感，克里斯托弗就再也不会收留她进他的家门……她也相当有把握，她那场私奔没有招出什么闲话。动身那天瑟斯顿少校在车站，可他们没有交谈——况且瑟斯顿是个挺像样的、留棕色小胡子的家伙，属于不传闲话的那一路人。
脱身还真费了点事，因为好几个星期里，佩罗恩看她就跟疯人院的看护看着病人似的。可他终于想通了一点：她绝不会撇下她的裙子走。于是有一天，一顿午饭灌下不少本地出产的火辣利口酒，饭后困得死去活来，他放她独自出门散步去了……
---
到那时她已经腻烦男人了……或者自以为腻烦了；因为眼见周围的女人为着一些最上不得台面的货色闹得人仰马翻，她实在不敢把话说死。反正男人从不兑现期望。相识之后也许比看上去有趣些；可是跟一个男人好上，几乎总像读一本你读过却忘了读过的书。跟任何一个男人亲近不上十分钟，你就会说：“这些我从前全读过……”开头你知道，中间你已腻烦，尤其结尾，你一清二楚……
她想起多年以前，她存心吓一吓她母亲的忏悔神父康塞特神父——他们前不久在爱尔兰把神父谋杀了，同卡斯门特一道……那位可怜的圣徒丝毫没有被吓着。他反将她一军。因为当她说了类似这样的话——说她理想中的“快活”日子——那年头人们爱说“快活”——就是每个周末跟一个不同的男人跑掉，神父对她说，要不了多久，等到那可怜亲爱的小伙子还在买车票的时候，她就已经腻烦了……
而且，天哪，他说对了……因为细想起来，从那位可怜的圣徒在德国那处小温泉——必定叫洛布沙伊德——她母亲的起居室里说出那句话的那天起，在烛光里，他的影子在四壁上声讨她，直到如今，她坐在这家砖砌棕榈色调的旅馆里——为了庆祝开战刚粉刷得雪白——从来没有一次，她跟哪个男人同坐过一列火车，而那男人有什么权利自认可以对她毛手毛脚……她不知道，康塞特神父坐在天上的位子上，俯瞰着这间休憩厅里的她，可会觉得满意……或许，真正促成她这场转变的，就是他……
一次也没有——直到昨天……因为昨天，那倒霉的佩罗恩或许还算沾着一星半点的权利，有大约两分钟——在她把他冻成一个透不过气、翻着金鱼眼的惨白雪人之前——变成男人在火车上变成的那副十足的讨厌相……胆子太大，可又蠢笨得不成人样，生怕列车员从车窗外张望进来，车速六十多英里，车厢没有通道……不，再也不要了，神父，她对着天花板把声音送上去……
天底下到底为什么就找不着一个男人，能跟你出走，而又偏偏只是——哦，一场轻喜剧——整整一个，整整一个有福的周末。整整一生一世，有福的一生……为什么不能呢？……想想吧……一生一世，跟一个好脾气的人，可他嗓音又不发咕噜，眼睛又不瞪成鳕鱼，浑身又不乱颤——不至于被要车票时连车票都摸不出来……亲爱的神父，她又朝上说，我要能找到那样的男人，那简直就是天堂……天堂里不嫁不娶……可是，当然，她近乎听天由命地想下去，他不会对你忠实……那也只得由他……
她猛地在椅子里坐直，吓得旁边的佩罗恩少校也差点从藤椅里跳起来，问是不是他回来了……她喊道：
“不，我就是被打入地狱也不肯……我肯才怪，我肯才怪，我肯才怪……决不。决不。凭永生的上帝起誓！”
她恶狠狠地问那慌了神的少校：
“克里斯托弗在这城里有没有女人？……你最好说实话！”
少校咕哝道：
“他……没有……他木头人一个……他连苏泽特那儿都从来不去……只去过一回，去捞出一个砸阿尔德洛妈妈家具的小毛孩子少尉……”
他嘟囔道：
“可你不该这么吓人跳！……要迁就点儿，是你说的……”他接着嘟囔，说她自打去了一趟伊桑格-莱佩尔旺什，举止一点没长进……又接着告诉她，法语里 yeux des pervenches 这个词儿，意思是长春花蓝的眼睛。这是他会的唯一一句法语，因为火车上碰到的一个法国人告诉他的，他还一直想，倘若她的眼睛是长春花蓝的……“可你没在听……我说这可算不上客气，”他咕咕哝哝地收了尾……
她在椅子里前倾着身子，手还攥着拳头抵在下巴底下，心里想着：也许克里斯托弗把瓦伦丁·万诺普安顿在这城里。也许这才是他甘愿留下来的缘故。她问：
“克里斯托弗为什么赖在这个上帝抛弃的鬼窝里？……他们管这儿叫‘不光彩的基地’……”
“因为他非待着不可呗……”佩罗恩少校说，“他得怎么吩咐怎么来……”
她说：“克里斯托弗！……你是说，像克里斯托弗这样的人，他们也能把他扣在他不想待的地方……”
“他敢走，他们不把他收拾得体无完肤才怪，”佩罗恩少校嚷道……“你当你那位宝贝是什么人？……英格兰国王？……”他又陡然阴狠地添了一句：“他要开小差，他们照样毙他……你说是不是？”
她说：“可这些都拦不住他在这城里养个女人吧？”
“反正他没有，”佩罗恩说，“他守着他那座宝贝老营房，活像只抱臭蛋的宝贝母鸡……人家就这么说他的……那家伙的事我一概不知……”
她阴恻恻地、懒洋洋地听着，觉得在他那哼哼唧唧的语调里，捉到了一丝当年伊桑格卧室里他嗓音底下惯有的那股杀人的疯劲儿。这家伙身上，的确带着几分治安法庭上那种闷葫芦似的疯杀人犯的气息。她突然起劲地想：
“要是他试试谋杀克里斯托弗呢……”她想象丈夫把他那脊梁搁在膝盖上折断，这念头掠过她的脑海，如火光穿过猫眼石。随后，喉咙发干，她对自己说：
“我非得查清楚那丫头是不是在鲁昂不可……”男人是抱团的。佩罗恩这家伙很可能在护着蒂特金斯。要说军队里哪条规矩能拴住克里斯托弗待在那地方，不可想象。上流人是关不住的。佩罗恩要是有脑子，就该明白，护着蒂特金斯，恰恰是得不到她的路数……可他没脑子……再说，性的阶级情谊是可怕地牢固……她知道自己就决不会为了得到自己的男人，出卖女人的秘密。那么……她怎样才能查明那丫头在不在城里？怎么查？……她想象蒂特金斯夜夜回她的家……可今夜他要来跟她过……这个她知道……同一个屋顶底下……从另一个女人身上刚下来……
她想象他此刻就在那儿……在城里高处、从电车上望得见的那种小别墅的客厅里……这会儿，他们准是在议论她……她整个人在椅子里拧着，一块肌肉压着一块肌肉地拧……她必须查出来……可是你怎么查？对抗一张天罗地网的同谋……这场战争整个儿是座“博爱居”……你想强暴数不清的女人，就投身战争……战争就是为这个的……所有这些男人，挤在这方寸之地……她站起身：
“我去，”她说，“为萨克塞夫人的宴会扑点粉……你不想待就请便……”她要盯住她看见的每一张脸，直到它招出秘密：克里斯托弗把万诺普家那丫头藏在这城里的什么地方……她想象那张长雀斑的塌鼻子脸贴在他腮上——“压”上去才对……她要去侦查……

### II / 二
===Original===

She found an early opportunity to carry on her investigations. For, at dinner that night, she found herself, Tietjens having gone to the telephone with a lance-corporal, opposite what she took to be a small tradesman, with fresh-coloured cheeks, and a great, grey, forward-sprouting moustache, in a uniform so creased that the creases resembled the veins of a leaf﻿ ﻿… A very trustworthy small tradesman: the grocer from round the corner whom, sometimes, you allow to supply you with paraffin﻿ ﻿… He was saying to her:
“If, ma’am, you multiply two-thousand nine hundred and something by ten you arrive at twenty-nine thousand odd﻿ ﻿…”
And she had exclaimed:
“You really mean that my husband, Captain Tietjens, spent yesterday afternoon in examining twenty-nine thousand toenails﻿ ﻿… And two thousand nine hundred toothbrushes﻿ ﻿…”
“I told him,” her interlocutor answered with deep seriousness, “that these being Colonial troops it was not so necessary to examine their toothbrushes﻿ ﻿… Imperial troops *will* use the brush they clean their buttons with for their teeth so as to have a clean toothbrush to show the medical officer﻿ ﻿…”
“It sounds,” she said with a little shudder, “as if you were all schoolboys playing a game﻿ ﻿… And you say my husband really occupies his mind with such things﻿ ﻿…”
Second-Lieutenant Cowley, dreadfully conscious that the shoulder-strap of his Sam Browne belt, purchased that afternoon at the Ordnance, and therefore brand-new, did not match the abdominal part of the belt that he had had for nearly ten years﻿—a splendid bit of leather, that!﻿—answered nevertheless stoutly:
“Madam! If the brains of an army aren’t, the life of an army *is*﻿ ﻿… in its feet﻿ ﻿… And nowadays, the medical officers say, in its teeth﻿ ﻿… Your husband, ma’am, is an admirable officer﻿ ﻿… He says that no draft he turns out shall﻿ ﻿…”
She said:
“He spent three hours in﻿ ﻿… You say, foot and kit inspection﻿ ﻿…”
Second-Lieutenant Cowley said:
“Of course he had other officers to help him with the kit﻿ ﻿… but he looked at every foot himself﻿ ﻿…”
She said:
“That took him from two till five﻿ ﻿… Then he had tea, I suppose﻿ ﻿… And went to﻿ ﻿… What is it?﻿ ﻿… The papers of the draft﻿ ﻿…”
Second-Lieutenant Cowley said, muffled through his moustache:
“If the captain is a little remiss in writing letters﻿ ﻿… I *have* heard﻿ ﻿… You might, madam﻿ ﻿… I’m a married man myself﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她很快便找到机会继续她的查访。因为那晚用晚餐时，蒂特金斯跟一名下士去接电话了，她对席坐的是个她一眼认作小店主的人——脸色红润，一大撇灰白胡子朝前扎煞着，军装皱得厉害，皱褶宛如叶脉……一个极其可靠的小店主：街角那家杂货铺的掌柜，你有时会容他给你送煤油……他正对她说：
"夫人，您要是拿两千九百多乘以十，就得出两万九千多……"
她惊呼起来：
"您当真是说，我丈夫蒂特金斯上尉昨天一下午都在检阅两万九千个脚指甲……外加两千九百把牙刷……"
"我跟他说过了，"她的交谈对象一脸肃穆地回答，"这些既是殖民地部队，牙刷倒不必查得那么严……帝国部队*偏要*拿擦纽扣的刷子刷牙，好省出一把干净的牙刷给军医官看……"
"听上去，"她微微打了个寒颤，"就像你们全是玩游戏的学生仔……你是说我丈夫当真把心思耗在这种事情上……"
考利少尉正痛切地意识到，他那条下午刚在军需处买的萨姆·布朗武装带肩带——因此崭新——与他用了近十年的腰带肚段不相配——那可是一段好皮子！——却依旧理直气壮地答道：
"夫人！一支军队的头脑即使算不得什么，军队的命脉*却*在……它的脚上……如今军医官们说，还在它的牙齿上……您丈夫，夫人，是位了不起的军官……他说过，他放走的每一批兵员都绝不……"
她说：
"他花了三个钟头……你说的是，脚与装备检查……"
考利少尉说：
"装备自然有别的军官帮着查……可每一双脚他都是亲自看的……"
她说：
"那把他从两点拴到了五点……然后他就用茶了，我料想……接着去……去哪儿来着？……办兵员的文书……"
考利少尉说，声音闷在胡子底下：
"上尉若是写信稍有怠慢……我*倒是*听说过……夫人，您不妨……我自己也是有家室的人……"

===Original===

with a daughter﻿ ﻿… And the army is not very good at writing letters﻿ ﻿… You might say, in that respect, that thank God we have got a navy, ma’am﻿ ﻿…”
She let him stagger on for a sentence or two, imagining that, in his confusion, she might come upon traces of Miss Wannop in Rouen. Then she said handsomely:
“Of course you have explained everything, Mr. Cowley, and I am very much obliged﻿ ﻿… Of course my husband would not have time to write very full letters﻿ ﻿… He is not like the giddy young subalterns who run after﻿ ﻿…”
He exclaimed in a great roar of laughter:
“The captain run after skirts﻿ ﻿… Why, I can number on my hands the times he’s been out of my sight since he’s had the battalion!”
A deep wave of depression went over Sylvia.
“Why,” Lieutenant Cowley laughed on, “if we *had* a laugh against him it was that he mothered the lot of us as if he was a hen sitting on addled eggs﻿ ﻿… For it’s only a ragtime army, as the saying is, when you’ve said the best for it that you can﻿ ﻿… And look at the other commanding officers we’ve had before we had him﻿ ﻿… There was Major Brooks﻿ ﻿… Never up before noon, if then, and out of camp by two-thirty. Get your returns ready for signing before then or never get ’em signed﻿ ﻿… And Colonel Potter﻿ ﻿… Bless my soul﻿ ﻿… ’e wouldn’t sign any blessed papers at all﻿ ﻿… He lived down here in this hotel, and we never saw him up at the camp at all﻿ ﻿… But the captain﻿ ﻿… We always say that﻿ ﻿… if ’e was a Chelsea adjutant getting off a draft of the Second Coldstreams﻿ ﻿…”
With her indolent and gracious beauty﻿—Sylvia knew that she was displaying indolent and gracious beauty﻿—Sylvia leaned over the tablecloth listening for items in the terrible indictment that, presently, she was going to bring against Tietjens﻿ ﻿… For the morality of these matters is this:﻿ ﻿… If you have an incomparably beautiful woman on your hands you must occupy yourself solely with her﻿ ﻿… Nature exacts that of you﻿ ﻿… until you are unfaithful to her with a snubnosed girl with freckles: that, of course, being a reaction, is still in a way occupying yourself with your woman!﻿ ﻿… But to betray her with a battalion﻿ ﻿… That is against decency, against Nature﻿ ﻿… And for him, Christopher Tietjens, to come down to the level of the men you met here!﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

"……还有个女儿……军队本来就不大擅长写信……在那一层上您可以说，感谢上帝我们还有海军，夫人……"
她任他磕磕绊绊再说上一两句，指望他在慌乱中或许会露出万诺普小姐在鲁昂的踪迹。随后她雍容大度地说：
"当然，您把一切都讲明白了，考利先生，我很承您的情……当然，我丈夫不会有工夫写长信……他可不比那些轻浮的年轻副官，追在……"
他爆出一阵大笑，吼道：
"上尉追女人……嘿，打他带这个营起，他离开我眼皮底下的次数，扳着指头都数得过来！"
一阵深重的沮丧漫过西尔维娅。
"可不是嘛，"考利少尉接着笑，"要说咱们背后笑话过他什么，那就是他把咱们一窝端地当崽子护着，活像母鸡孵一窝臭蛋……因为这军队啊，俗话说得好，任你往好里夸到头，也不过是支拉格泰姆杂牌军……再瞧瞧他之前咱们挨过的那些指挥官……有位布鲁克斯少校……晌午前从不起床，起来也够呛，两点半人就出了营。报表要他签字，赶在那之前备妥，否则永无签字之日……还有波特上校……老天爷……他什么鬼文件都不签……他就住在这家旅馆里，营里压根见不着他影……可是上尉……我们总念叨……他要是切尔西的副官，打发第二冷溪团的一批兵……"
西尔维娅带着慵懒华贵的美——她知道自己正在展露慵懒华贵的美——俯在桌布上，捕捉那份可怕罪状录里的一条条款项，那份罪状录她随后就要用来控告蒂特金斯……因为这类事的道德律是这样的：……你手中若有一位绝色美人，你就必须只围着她一个人转……天性如此勒令你……直到你背叛她，恋上一个长雀斑的狮子鼻姑娘：那当然属于反激，某种意义上仍是在围着你的女人转！……可是为了一个营背叛她……那就是逆体面、逆天性而行……而他，克里斯托弗·蒂特金斯，竟堕落到与你在这一带碰见的这路男人为伍的地步！……

===Original===

Tietjens, mooning down the room between tables, had more than his usually aloof air since he had just come out of a telephone box. He slipped, a weary mass, into the polished chair between her and the lieutenant. He said:
“I’ve got the washing arranged for﻿ ﻿…” and Sylvia gave to herself a little hiss between the teeth, of vindictive pleasure! This was indeed betrayal to a battalion. He added: “I shall have to be up in camp before four-thirty tomorrow morning﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia could not resist saying:
“Isn’t there a poem﻿ ﻿… ‘Ah me, the dawn, the dawn, it comes too soon!’﻿ ﻿… said of course by lovers in bed?﻿ ﻿… Who was the poet?”
Cowley went visibly red to the roots of his hair and evidently beyond. Tietjens finished his speech to Cowley, who had remonstrated against his going up to the camp so early by saying that he had not been able to get hold of an officer to march the draft. He then said in his leisurely way:
“There were a great many poems with that refrain in the Middle Ages﻿ ﻿… You are probably thinking of an albade by Arnaut Daniel, which someone translated lately﻿ ﻿… An albade was a song to be sung at dawn when, presumably, no one but lovers would be likely to sing﻿ ﻿…”
“Will there,” Sylvia asked, “be anyone but you singing up in your camp tomorrow at four?”
She could not help it﻿ ﻿… She knew that Tietjens had adopted his slow pomposity in order to give the grotesque object at the table with them time to recover from his confusion. She hated him for it. What right had he to make himself appear a pompous ass in order to shield the confusion of anybody?
The second-lieutenant came out of his confusion to exclaim, actually slapping his thigh:
“There you are, madam﻿ ﻿… Trust the captain to know everything!﻿ ﻿… I don’t believe there’s a question under the sun you could ask him that he couldn’t answer﻿ ﻿… They say up at the camp﻿ ﻿…” He went on with long stories of all the questions Tietjens *had* answered up at the camp﻿ ﻿…
Emotion was going all over Sylvia﻿ ﻿… at the proximity of Tietjens. She said to herself: “Is this to go on forever?” Her hands were ice-cold. She touched the back of her left hand with the fingers of her right. It *was* ice-cold. She looked at her hands. They were bloodless﻿ ﻿… She said to herself: “It’s pure sexual passion﻿ ﻿… it’s pure sexual passion﻿ ﻿… God! Can’t I get over this?” She said: “Father!﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

蒂特金斯从一排桌间恍恍惚惚踱过来，刚出电话亭的他比平日更添几分疏离。他像一堆疲惫的肉，滑进她与少尉之间那把锃亮的椅子。他说：
"洗澡的事我安排好了……"西尔维娅从齿缝里对自己轻轻嘶了一声，带着泄愤的快意！这真是对一个营的背叛。他又说："明早四点半之前我就得赶到营里……"
西尔维娅忍不住说：
"不是有一首诗么……'唉，这黎明，黎明，来得何其匆匆！'……自然是在床笫间的恋人说的？……诗人是谁？"
考利红到了发根，明摆着还不止发根。蒂特金斯先把对考利说的话说完——考利方才不赞成他这么早上营，说是寻不到军官带兵员行军。然后他不紧不慢地说：
"中世纪有许多诗以那句叠句收尾……您想到的多半是阿诺·丹尼尔的一首晨歌，近年有人译过……晨歌是黎明时分唱的歌，想必除了恋人，谁也不会在那个时辰开口……"
"明早四点，"西尔维娅问，"你们营里除了你，还有别人会唱歌吗？"
她按捺不住……她知道蒂特金斯故意端起慢吞吞的夫子腔，好给同桌那个滑稽家伙留出从窘态里缓过气的时间。她恨他这一点。他有什么权利把自己扮成一头自负的蠢驴，去遮掩任何人的窘态？
少尉从窘态里缓过来，竟一拍大腿喊道：
"您瞧见了吧，夫人……上尉什么都懂，只管放心！……我不信天底下有什么问题问得倒他……营里人都说……"他接着长篇大论，讲起蒂特金斯在营里*曾经*对答如流的种种问题……
情绪漫过西尔维娅全身……只因蒂特金斯近在咫尺。她对自己说："这要永远这样下去么？"她的双手冰凉。她用右手指尖碰碰左手手背。*当真*冰凉。她看看自己这双手。毫无血色……她对自己说："这是纯粹的肉欲……纯粹的肉欲……上帝啊！我就熬不过去么？"她说："神父啊！……"

===Original===

You used to be fond of Christopher﻿ ﻿… *Get* our Lady to get me over this﻿ ﻿… It’s the ruin of him and the ruin of me. But, oh *damn*, don’t!﻿ ﻿… For it’s all I have to live for﻿ ﻿…” She said: “When he came mooning back from the telephone I thought it was all right﻿ ﻿… I thought what a heavy wooden-horse he looked﻿ ﻿… For two minutes﻿ ﻿… Then it’s all over me again﻿ ﻿… I want to swallow my saliva and I can’t. My throat won’t work﻿ ﻿…”
She leaned one of her white bare arms on the tablecloth towards the walrus-moustache that was still snuffling gloriously:
“They used to call him Old Sol at school,” she said. “But there’s one question of Solomon’s he could not answer﻿ ﻿… The one about the way of a man with﻿ ﻿… Oh, a maid!﻿ ﻿… Ask him what happened before the dawn ninety-six﻿—no, ninety-eight days ago﻿ ﻿…”
She said to herself: “I can’t help it﻿ ﻿… Oh, I *can’t* help it﻿ ﻿…”
The ex-sergeant-major was exclaiming happily:
“Oh, no one ever said the captain was one of these thought-readers﻿ ﻿… It’s real solid knowledge of men and things he has﻿ ﻿… Wonderful how he knows the men considering he was not born in the service﻿ ﻿… But there, your born gentleman mixes with men all his days and knows them. Down to the ground and inside their puttees﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens was looking straight in front of him, his face perfectly expressionless.
“But I bet I got him﻿ ﻿…” she said to herself and then to the sergeant-major:
“I suppose now an army officer﻿—one of your born gentlemen﻿—when a back-from-leave train goes out from any of the great stations﻿—Paddington, say﻿—to the front﻿ ﻿… He knows how all the men are feeling﻿ ﻿… But not what the married women think﻿ ﻿… or the﻿ ﻿… the girl﻿ ﻿…”
She said to herself: “Damn it, how clumsy I am getting!﻿ ﻿… I used to be able to take his hide off with a word. Now I take sentences at a time﻿ ﻿…”
She went on with her uninterrupted sentence to Cowley: “Of course he may never be going to see his only son again, so it makes him sensitive﻿ ﻿… The officer at Paddington, I mean﻿ ﻿…”
She said to herself: “By God, if that beast does not give in to me tonight he never *shall* see Michael again﻿ ﻿… Ah, but I got him﻿ ﻿… Tietjens had his eyes closed, round each of his high-coloured nostrils a crescent of whiteness was beginning. And increasing﻿ ﻿… She felt a sudden alarm and held the edge of the table with her extended arm to steady herself﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

"……您从前是疼克里斯托弗的……*求*圣母替我熬过这一关……这是他的毁灭，也是我的毁灭。可是，哦，*该死*，别这样！……这是我活在世上的全部指望……"她说："他从电话旁恍恍惚惚走回来那阵，我还当没事了……我想，他瞧上去多像一匹沉重的木马……有两分钟……随后那感觉又把我整个罩住……我想咽口唾沫，咽不下。喉咙不管用了……"
她把一条白皙的光臂支上桌布，伸向那撮仍在响亮抽鼻子的海象胡：
"他在学校里人家叫他老索尔，"她说，"可有所罗门的一个问题他答不上……就是男人怎样同……哦，同少女的那一条！……问问他，九十六——不，九十八天前的黎明之前发生了什么……"
她对自己说："我没法子……哦，我*没法子*……"
前军士长正喜不自胜地感叹：
"哦，从没人说上尉是那种读心的人……他对人对事有的是实打实的学问……想想他并非行伍出身，却这般懂兵，真是奇了……不过话说回来，你们天生的绅士一辈子同各色人厮混，自然懂他们。懂到骨头缝里，懂进绑腿里头……"
蒂特金斯直望前方，脸上全无表情。
"可我打赌我逮着了他……"她对自己说，随即对军士长说：
"我想请问，一位陆军军官——你们那种天生的绅士——当一列休假期满的火车从某座大站开出——比如帕丁顿——开往前线……弟兄们心里什么滋味，他全知道……可已婚妇人心里想什么，他不知道……还有那些……那些姑娘……"
她对自己说："见鬼，我变得多笨！……从前我一个字就能剥下他的皮。如今得一整句一整句地来……"
她接着对考利说那句不曾间断的话："当然，他也许此生再见不到他的独子了，所以心肠才软了……我是说帕丁顿站上的那位军官……"
她对自己说："上帝在上，那畜生今晚若不依我，他就永远*休想*再见到迈克尔……啊，可我逮着了他……"蒂特金斯双目阖着，两片深色鼻孔四周各泛起一弯月牙白。而且在扩大……她猛地一阵惊惶，伸出胳膊扣住桌沿稳住自己……

===Original===

Men went white at the nose like that when they were going to faint﻿ ﻿… She did not want him to faint﻿ ﻿… But he *had* noticed the word Paddington﻿ ﻿… Ninety-eight days before﻿ ﻿… She had counted every day since﻿ ﻿… She had got that much information﻿ ﻿… She had said *Paddington* outside the house at dawn and he had taken it as a farewell. He *had*﻿ ﻿… He had imagined himself free to do what he liked with the girl﻿ ﻿… Well, he wasn’t﻿ ﻿… That was why he was white about the gills﻿ ﻿…”
Cowley exclaimed loudly:
“Paddington!﻿ ﻿… It isn’t from there that back-from-leave trains go. Not for the front: the B.E.F.﻿ ﻿… Not from Paddington﻿ ﻿… The Glamorganshires go from there to the depot﻿ ﻿… And the Liverpools﻿ ﻿… They’ve got a depot at Birkenhead﻿ ﻿… Or is that the Cheshires?﻿ ﻿…” He asked of Tietjens: “Is it the Liverpools or the Cheshires that have a depot at Birkenhead, sir?﻿ ﻿… You remember we recruited a draft from there when we were at Penhally﻿ ﻿… At any rate, you go to Birkenhead from Paddington﻿ ﻿… I was never there myself﻿ ﻿… They say it’s a nice place﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia said﻿—she did not want to say it:
“It’s quite a nice place﻿ ﻿… but I should not think of staying there forever﻿ ﻿…”
Tietjens said:
“The Cheshires have a training camp﻿—not a depot﻿—near Birkenhead. And of course there are R.G.A.s there﻿ ﻿…” She had been looking away from him﻿ ﻿… Cowley exclaimed:
“You were nearly off, sir,” hilariously. “You had your peepers shut﻿ ﻿…” Lifting a champagne glass, he inclined himself towards her. “You must excuse the captain, ma’am,” he said. “He had no sleep last night﻿ ﻿… Largely owing to my fault﻿ ﻿… Which is what makes it so kind of him﻿ ﻿… I tell you, ma’am, there are few things I would not do for the captain﻿ ﻿…” He drank his champagne and began an explanation: “You may not know, ma’am, this is a great day for me﻿ ﻿… And you and the captain are making it the greatest day of my life﻿ ﻿…” Why, at four this morning there hadn’t been a wretcheder man in Ruin town﻿ ﻿… And now﻿ ﻿… He must tell her that he suffered from an unfortunate﻿—a miserable﻿—complaint﻿ ﻿… One that makes one have to be careful of celebrations﻿ ﻿… And today was a day that he had to celebrate﻿ ﻿… But he dare not have done it where Sergeant-Major Ledoux is along with a lot of their old mates﻿ ﻿… “I dare not﻿ ﻿… I dussn’t!” he finished﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

人要昏厥时，鼻子就是那样发白的……她不要他昏厥……可他*确实*听见了帕丁顿这个词……九十八天前……此后她一天天数着……这一丁点情报她拿到了……黎明时分她在宅子门外说了声*帕丁顿*，他把它当成了诀别。他*确实*……他自以为从此可以对那姑娘为所欲为……哼，他不能……所以他腮边才发白……"
考利高声嚷道：
"帕丁顿！……休假回前线的火车不从那儿开。不去前线的：英国远征军……不从帕丁顿开……格拉摩根郡团打那儿去补给站……还有利物浦团……他们在伯肯黑德有个补给站……还是说那是柴郡团的？……"他问蒂特金斯："长官，在伯肯黑德设补给站的，是利物浦团还是柴郡团？……您记得咱们在彭哈利那阵，从那儿补过一批兵……不管怎么说，打帕丁顿能去伯肯黑德……我自己没去过……听说是个好地方……"
西尔维娅说——她并不想说的：
"那地方挺好……可我没打算在那儿待一辈子……"
蒂特金斯说："柴郡团在伯肯黑德附近有个训练营——不是补给站。那儿当然还驻着皇家炮兵……"她一直偏着脸不看他……考利欢快地喊道："长官，您差点睡着喽。眼皮都阖上了……"他举起一杯香槟，朝她倾过身来。"您得多担待上尉，夫人，"他说。"他昨儿夜没合眼……多半是我的错……所以他这份情义才重……我跟您说，夫人，为了上尉，几乎没有我不肯干的事……"他饮了香槟，开始解释："您未必知道，夫人，今天是我的大日子……而您和上尉，把它变成了我这辈子顶大的日子……"您想，今儿凌晨四点，鲁昂这破城里还没有比他更潦倒的人……可如今……他必须告诉她，他患着一种不幸的——要命的——病……一种叫人逢庆祝便不得不处处当心的病……而今天偏是他必须庆祝的日子……可他不敢在勒杜军士长同一帮老伙计跟前庆祝……"我不敢……俺不敢！"他这样收了尾……

“So I might have been sitting, now, at this very moment, up in the cold camp﻿ ﻿… But for you and the captain﻿ ﻿… Up in the cold camp﻿ ﻿… You’ll excuse me, ma’am﻿ ﻿…”
Sylvia felt that her lids were suddenly wavering:
“I might have been myself,” she said, “in a cold camp, too﻿ ﻿… if I hadn’t thrown myself on the captain’s mercy!﻿ ﻿… At Birkenhead, you know﻿ ﻿… I happened to be there till three weeks ago﻿ ﻿… It’s strange that you mentioned it﻿ ﻿… There *are* things like signs﻿ ﻿… but you’re not a Catholic! They could hardly be coincidences﻿ ﻿…”
She was trembling﻿ ﻿… She looked, fumblingly opening it, into the little mirror of her powder-box﻿—of chased, very thin gold with a small blue stone, like a forget-me-not in the centre of the concentric engravings﻿ ﻿… Drake﻿—the possible father of Michael﻿—had given it to her﻿ ﻿… The first thing he had ever given her. She had brought it down tonight out of defiance. She imagined that Tietjens disliked it﻿ ﻿… She said breathlessly to herself: “perhaps the damn thing is an ill omen﻿ ﻿…” Drake had been the first man who had ever﻿ ﻿… A hot-breathed brute!﻿ ﻿… In the little glass her features were chalk-white﻿ ﻿… She looked like﻿ ﻿… she looked like﻿ ﻿… She had a dress of golden tissue﻿ ﻿… The breath was short between her white set teeth﻿ ﻿… Her face was as white as her teeth﻿ ﻿… And﻿ ﻿… Yes! Nearly! Her lips﻿ ﻿… What was her face like?﻿ ﻿… In the chapel of the convent of Birkenhead there was a tomb all of alabaster﻿ ﻿… She said to herself:
“He was near fainting﻿ ﻿… I’m near fainting﻿ ﻿… What’s this beastly thing that’s between us?﻿ ﻿… If I let myself faint﻿ ﻿… But it would not make that beast’s face any less wooden!﻿ ﻿…”
She leaned across the table and patted the ex-sergeantmajor’s black-haired hand:
“I’m sure,” she said, “you’re a very good man﻿ ﻿…” She did not try to keep the tears out of her eyes, remembering his words: “Up in the cold camp.”﻿ ﻿… “I’m glad the captain, as you call him, did not leave you in the cold camp﻿ ﻿… You’re devoted to him, aren’t you?﻿ ﻿… There are others he does leave﻿ ﻿… up in﻿ ﻿… the cold camp﻿ ﻿… For punishment, you know﻿ ﻿…”
The ex-sergeant-major, the tears in his eyes too, said:
“Well, there *is* men you ’as to give the C.B. to﻿ ﻿… C.B. means confined to barracks﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh, there are!” she exclaimed. “There are!﻿ ﻿… And women, too﻿ ﻿… Surely there are women, too?﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

"这么说来，这会儿此时此刻，我本该还蹲在上头冷冰冰的营房里……多亏您和上尉……上头冷冰冰的营房……您多包涵，夫人……"
西尔维娅觉得眼皮忽然颤动起来：
"我也一样，"她说，"也本该蹲在冷营房里……要不是我把自身托付给了上尉的慈悲！……在伯肯黑德，你是知道的……我碰巧三周前还待在那儿……真蹊跷，你偏偏提起它……世上*确有*征兆这样的事……可你又不是天主教徒！总不会全是巧合……"
她在发抖……她哆哆嗦嗦打开粉盒，往小镜里瞧——粉盒是雕花薄金打的，正中嵌一粒小蓝石，像同心刻纹中央一朵勿忘我……德雷克——迈克尔那个可能的父亲——送她的……他送她的头一件东西。今晚她把它带下楼来，存心挑衅。她料定蒂特金斯见不得它……她气喘不定地对自己说："这该死的东西，怕是个凶兆……"德雷克是头一个曾……一头热气喷鼻的畜生！……小镜里她的面容白如粉笔……她像……她像……她穿一身金纱裙……雪白整齐的齿间气息短促……脸白得同牙齿一样……而且……对！快了！她的唇……她这张脸到底像什么？……伯肯黑德修院的小堂里，有一座通体雪花石膏的墓……她对自己说：
"他方才险些昏过去……我也快昏过去了……横在我们中间的这鬼东西是什么？……我若由自己昏过去……可那也软化不了那畜生的木头脸分毫！……"
她隔桌俯过身去，拍拍前军士长那只黑毛大手：
"我敢保，"她说，"您是个大好人……"她想起他那句"上头冷冰冰的营房"，索性由泪水涨在眼里……"我很高兴上尉——照你的叫法——没把你撂在冷营房里……你一心向着他，是吧？……另有一些人，他可是撂下了……撂在上头……冷营房里……当惩罚，你懂的……"
前军士长眼里也噙着泪，说：
"唉，*总有*些弟兄，你不得不关他的C.B.……C.B.就是禁闭营房……"
"哦，有的是！"她喊道。"有的是！……女人也一样……女人肯定也有受禁的，对不对？……"

===Original===

The sergeant-major said:
“Wacks, per’aps﻿ ﻿… I don’t know﻿ ﻿… They say women’s discipline is like ours﻿ ﻿… Founded on hours!”
She said:
“Do you know what they used to say of the captain?﻿ ﻿…” She said to herself: “I pray to God the stiff, fatuous beast likes sitting here listening to this stuff﻿ ﻿… Blessed Virgin, mother of God, make him take me﻿ ﻿… Before midnight. Before eleven﻿ ﻿… As soon as we get rid of this﻿ ﻿… No, he’s a decent little man﻿ ﻿… Blessed Virgin!”﻿ ﻿… “Do you know what they used to say of the captain?﻿ ﻿… I heard the warmest banker in England say it of him﻿ ﻿…”
The sergeant-major, his eyes enormously opened, said:
“Did you know the warmest banker in England?﻿ ﻿… But there, we always knew the captain was well connected﻿ ﻿…” She went on:
“They said of him﻿ ﻿… He was always helping people.”﻿ ﻿… “Holy Mary, mother of God!﻿ ﻿… He’s my *husband*﻿ ﻿… It’s not a sin﻿ ﻿… Before midnight﻿ ﻿… Oh, give me a sign﻿ ﻿… Or before﻿ ﻿… the termination of hostilities﻿ ﻿… If you give me a sign I could wait.”﻿ ﻿… “He helped virtuous Scotch students, and broken-down gentry﻿ ﻿… And women taken in adultery﻿ ﻿… All of them﻿ ﻿… Like﻿ ﻿… You know Who﻿ ﻿… That is his model﻿ ﻿…” She said to herself: “Curse him!﻿ ﻿… I hope he likes it﻿ ﻿… You’d think the only thing he thinks about is the beastly duck he’s wolfing down.”﻿ ﻿… And then aloud: “They used to say: ‘He saved others; himself he could not save﻿ ﻿…’ ”
The ex-sergeant-major looked at her gravely:
“Ma’am,” he said, “we couldn’t say exactly that of the captain﻿ ﻿… For I fancy it was said of our Redeemer﻿ ﻿… But we *’ave* said that if ever there was a poor bloke the captain could ’elp, ’elp ’im ’e would﻿ ﻿… Yet the unit was always getting ’ellish strafe from headquarters﻿ ﻿…”
Suddenly Sylvia began to laugh﻿ ﻿… As she began to laugh she had remembered﻿ ﻿… The alabaster image in the nun’s chapel at Birkenhead the vision of which had just presented itself to her, had been the recumbent tomb of an honourable Mrs. Tremayne-Warlock﻿ ﻿… She was said to have sinned in her youth﻿ ﻿… And her husband had never forgiven her﻿ ﻿… That was what the nuns said﻿ ﻿… She said aloud:
“A sign﻿ ﻿…” Then to herself: “Blessed Mary!﻿ ﻿… You’ve given it me in the neck﻿ ﻿… Yet you could not name a father for your child, and I can name two﻿ ﻿… I’m going mad﻿ ﻿… Both I and he are going to go mad﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

军士长说：
"鞭子吧，兴许……我说不准……人家说女人的纪律跟咱们一个样……建立在钟点上！"
她说："你可知道人家从前怎么说上尉？……"她对自己说："我求上帝叫这具僵死的、自命不凡的畜生爱坐在这儿听这些货色……有福的马利亚，天主之母，叫他要我……午夜之前。十一点之前……等咱们打发了这一位就走……不，他是个正派的小个子……圣母！"……"你可知道人家从前怎么说上尉？……我是听英格兰最热忱的银行家说起他的……"
军士长双眼瞪得溜圆，说：
"您认识英格兰最热忱的银行家？……怪不得，咱们一向知道上尉交游广阔……"她接着说：
"人家说他……他总是在帮人。"……"圣马利亚，天主之母！……他是我的*丈夫*……这不算罪……午夜之前……哦，给我一个征兆……不然就……停战之前……您给我一个征兆，我等得起。"……"他帮过品行端谨的苏格兰学生，帮过破落的世家子弟……也帮过行淫时被拿的妇人……个个都帮……就像……你知道的那一位……那是他的榜样……"她对自己说："该死的！……但愿他听得受用……瞧那样子，他一心惦记的只有他大口吞咽的那只讨厌鸭子。"……然后她出声说："人家从前常说：'他救了别人，却救不了自己……'"
前军士长正色望她：
"夫人，"他说，"上尉的事，咱们不能说得一模一样……我记得那话说的是我们的救主……不过咱们*确实*说过，只要有哪个可怜虫是上尉帮得上的，他准帮……可咱们这个单位还是老挨总部一顿臭骂……"
西尔维娅忽然笑起来……她一开始笑，便想起了……方才浮现在她眼前的伯肯黑德修女堂里那尊雪花石膏像，原是一位贵妇特雷梅恩-沃洛克夫人的卧像……据说她年少时失过足……而她丈夫至死没有饶她……修女们是这么说的……她出声道："一个征兆……"随即心里说："有福的马利亚！……您这一手可坑苦了我……可您给您孩子指不出一个父亲，我却指得出两个……我要疯了……他和我，我们俩都要疯了……"

===Original===

She thought of dashing an enormous patch of red upon either cheek. Then she thought it would be rather melodramatic﻿ ﻿…
---
She made in the smoking-room, whilst she was waiting for both Tietjens and Cowley to come back from the telephone, another pact﻿ ﻿… This time with Father Consett in heaven! She was fairly sure that Father Consett﻿—and quite possibly other of the heavenly powers﻿—wanted Christopher not to be worried, so that he could get on with the war﻿—or because he was a good sort of dullish man such as the heavenly authorities are apt to like﻿ ﻿… Something like that﻿ ﻿…
She was by that time fairly calm again. You cannot keep up fits of emotion by the hour: at any rate, with her, the fits of emotion were periodical and unexpected, though her colder passion remained always the same﻿ ﻿… Thus, when Christopher had come into Lady Sachse’s that afternoon, she had been perfectly calm. He had mooned through a number of officers, both French and English, in a great octagonal, bluish salon where Lady Sachse gave her teas, and had come to her side with just a nod﻿—the merest inflection of the head!﻿ ﻿… Perowne had melted away somewhere behind the disagreeable duchess. The general, very splendid and white-headed and scarlet-tipped and gilt, had also borne down upon her at that﻿ ﻿… At the sight of Perowne with her he had been sniffing and snorting whilst he talked to the young nobleman﻿—a dark fellow in blue with a new belt who seemed just a shade too theatrical, he being chauffeur to a marshal of France and first cousin and nearest relative, except for parents and grandparents, of the prospective bride﻿ ﻿…
